S-estimation in Linear Models with Structured Covariance Matrices This work has been partly supported by the French Agence Nationale de la Recherche through the Investments for the Future (Investissements d’Avenir) program, grant ANR-17-EURE-0010.
Hendrik Paul Lopuhaä, Valerie Gares, Anne Ruiz-Gazen

Abstract
We provide a unified approach to S-estimation in balanced linear models with structured covariance matrices. Of main interest are S-estimators for linear mixed effects models, but our approach also includes S-estimators in several other standard multivariate models, such as multiple regression, multivariate regression, and multivariate location and scatter. We provide sufficient conditions for the existence of S-functionals and S-estimators, establish asymptotic properties such as consistency and asymptotic normality, and derive their robustness properties in terms of breakdown point and influence function. All the results are obtained for general identifiable covariance structures and are established under mild conditions on the distribution of the observations, which goes far beyond models with elliptically contoured densities. Some of our results are new and others are more general than existing ones in the literature. In this way this manuscript completes and improves results on S-estimation in a wide variety of multivariate models. We illustrate our results by means of a simulation study and an application to data from a trial on the treatment of lead-exposed children.



1
Introduction

Linear models are widely used and provide a versatile approach for analyzing correlated responses,
such as longitudinal data, growth data or repeated measurements.
In such models, each subject
i
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, is observed at
k_{i}
occasions,
and the vector of responses
\mathbf{y}_{i}
is assumed to arise from the model

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}+\mathbf{u}_{i},

where
\mathbf{X}_{i}
is the design matrix for the
i
th subject and
\mathbf{u}_{i}
is a vector
whose covariance matrix can be used to model the correlation between the responses.
One possibility is the linear mixed effects model, in which the random effects together with the measurement error
yields a specific covariance structure depending on a vector
\bm{\theta}
consisting of some unknown covariance parameters.
Other covariance structures may arise, for example if the
\mathbf{u}_{i}
are the outcome of a time series,
see e.g.,
[
14
]
or
[
10
]
, for different possible covariance structures.

Maximum likelihood estimation of
\bm{\beta}
and
\bm{\theta}
has been studied, e.g., in
[
12
,
25
,
15
]
,
see also
[
10
,
7
]
.
To be resistant against outliers, robust methods have been investigated for linear mixed effects models, e.g.,
in
[
22
,
5
,
4
,
13
,
1
,
3
]
.
This mostly concerns S-estimators, originally introduced in the multiple regression context by Rousseeuw and Yohai
[
27
]

and extended to multivariate location and scatter in
[
6
,
17
]
, to multivariate regression in
[
29
]
,
and to linear mixed effects models
in
[
5
,
13
,
3
]
.
S-estimators are well known smooth versions of the minimum volume ellipsoid estimator
[
26
]
that
are highly resistant against outliers.
As such, S-estimators have gained popularity as robust estimators,
but they may also serve as initial estimators to further improve the efficiency.
However, the theory about these estimators is far from complete,
even in balanced models where the number of observed responses is the same for all subjects.

In view of this, we provide a unified approach to S-estimation in balanced linear models with structured covariance matrices,
and postpone a unified approach for unbalanced models to a future paper.
The balanced setup is already quite flexible and includes several specific multivariate statistical models.
Of main interest are S-estimators for linear mixed effects models, but our approach also includes
S-estimators in several other standard multivariate models, such as multiple regression,
multivariate regression, and multivariate location and scatter.
We provide sufficient conditions for the existence of S-functionals and S-estimators,
establish their asymptotic properties, such as consistency and asymptotic normality,
and derive their robustness properties in terms of breakdown point and influence function.
All results are obtained for a large class of identifiable covariance structures, and are established under very mild conditions
on the distribution of the observations, which goes far beyond models with elliptically contoured densities.
In this way, some of our results are new and others are more general than existing ones in the literature.

Existence of S-estimators and S-functionals is established under mild conditions.
Although existence of the estimators seems a basic requirement,
such results are missing for instance for multivariate regression in
[
30
]

and for linear mixed effects models in
[
5
,
3
]
.
We obtain robustness properties for S-estimators, such as breakdown point and influence function,
under mild conditions on collections of observations and under mild conditions on the distribution
of the observations.
High breakdown and a bounded influence function seem basic requirements for a robust method,
but both properties are not available for linear mixed effects models
[
5
,
3
]
.
For multivariate regression
[
30
]
, the influence function is only determined at distributions with an
elliptical contoured density.
Finally, we establish consistency and asymptotic normality for S-estimators under mild conditions on the distribution of the observations.
A rigorous derivation is missing for multivariate regression
[
30
]
, or is only available
for observations from a normal distribution
[
27
,
3
]
.

We apply our asymptotic results, such as influence function and asymptotic normality, to the special case for which the
distribution of the observations corresponds to an elliptically contoured density.
In this way we retrieve earlier results found in
[
27
,
17
,
30
]
.
Somewhat surprisingly, the asymptotic variances of our S-estimators for linear mixed effects models in which the response
has an elliptically contoured density, differ from the ones found in
[
5
]
.
We investigate this difference by means of a simulation study.

The paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we explain the model in detail and
provide some examples of standard multivariate models that are included in our setup.
In Section
3
we define the S-estimator and S-functional
and in Section
4
we give conditions under which they exist.
In Section
5
we establish continuity of the S-functional, which is then used to
obtain consistency of the S-estimator.
Section
6
deals with the breakdown point.
Section
7
provides the preparation for Sections
8

and
9
in which we obtain the influence function and establish asymptotic normality.
Finally, in Section
10
, we illustrate our results by means of a simulation
and investigate the performance of our estimators
by means of an application to data from a trial on the treatment of lead-exposed children.
All proofs and some technical lemmas are put in an Appendix at the end of the paper.
Other long and technical proofs are available as supplemental material
[
16
]
.

2
Balanced models with structured covariances

We consider independent observations
(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{X}_{n})
,
for which we assume the following model

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}+\mathbf{u}_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.1)

where
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
contains repeated measurements for the
i
-th subject,

\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
is an unknown parameter vector,

\mathbf{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times q}
is a known design matrix, and

\mathbf{u}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
are unobservable independent mean zero random vectors with
covariance matrix
\mathbf{V}\in\text{PDS}(k)
,
the class of positive definite symmetric
k\times k
matrices.
The model is balanced in the sense that all
\mathbf{y}_{i}
have the same dimension.
Furthermore, we consider a structured covariance matrix, that is,
the matrix
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is a known function of unknown covariance parameters combined in a vector
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
We first discuss some examples that are covered by this setup.

Example 1
.

An important case of interest is the (balanced) linear mixed effects model

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}+\mathbf{Z}\gamma_{i}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n.

(2.2)

This model arises from
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\mathbf{Z}\bm{\gamma}_{i}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i}
,
for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
where
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times g}
is known and
\bm{\gamma}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{g}
and
\bm{\epsilon}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
are independent mean zero random variables, with
unknown covariance matrices
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{R}
, respectively.
In this case
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{G}\mathbf{Z}^{T}+\mathbf{R}
and

\bm{\theta}=(\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{G})^{T},\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{R})^{T})^{T}
,
where

\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{A})=(a_{11},\ldots,a_{k1},a_{22},\ldots,a_{kk})

(2.3)

is the unique
k(k+1)/2
-vector that stacks the columns of the lower triangle elements of a symmetric matrix
\mathbf{A}
.
In full generality, the model is usually overparametrized and one may run into identifiability problems.
A more feasible example is obtained by taking
\mathbf{R}=\sigma_{0}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}
,

\mathbf{Z}=\left[\mathbf{Z}_{1}\,\cdots\,\mathbf{Z}_{r}\right]
and

\bm{\gamma}_{i}=(\gamma_{i1}\ldots,\gamma_{ir})^{T}
,
where the
\mathbf{Z}_{j}
’s are known
k\times g_{j}
design matrices and
the
\gamma_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{g_{j}}
are independent mean zero random variables with covariance matrix
\sigma_{j}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{g_{j}}
,
for
j=1,\ldots,r
.
This leads to

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{Z}_{j}\gamma_{ij}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.4)

with
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sigma_{j}^{2}\mathbf{Z}_{j}\mathbf{Z}_{j}^{T}+\sigma_{0}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}
and
\bm{\theta}=(\sigma_{0}^{2},\sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}^{2})
.

Example 2
.

An example with an unstructured covariance is
the multivariate linear regression model

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{B}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{i}+\mathbf{u}_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.5)

where
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times k}
is a matrix of unknown parameters,
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
is known,
and
\mathbf{u}_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, are independent mean zero random variables with
covariance matrix
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{C}\in\text{PDS}(k)
.
In this case, the vector of unknown covariance parameters is given by

\bm{\theta}=\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{C})=(c_{11},\ldots,c_{1k},c_{22},\ldots,c_{kk})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{\frac{1}{2}k(k+1)}.

(2.6)

The model can be obtained as a special case of (
2.1
), by taking

\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{k}
and
\bm{\beta}=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{B}^{T})
, where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product
and
\mathrm{vec}(\cdot)
is the
k^{2}
-vector that stacks the columns of a matrix.
Clearly, the linear multiple regression model is a special case with
k=1
.

Example 3
.

Model (
2.1
) also includes examples, for which

\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{n}
are generated from a time series.
One example, is the case where
\mathbf{u}_{i}
has a covariance matrix with elements

v_{st}=\sigma^{2}\rho^{|s-t|},\quad s,t=1,\ldots,k.

(2.7)

This arises when the
\mathbf{u}_{i}
’s are generated by an autoregressive process of order one.
The vector of unknown covariance parameters is
\bm{\theta}=(\sigma^{2},\rho)\in(0,\infty)\times[-1,1]
.
A general stationary process leads to

v_{st}=\theta_{|s-t|+1},\quad s,t=1,\ldots,k,

(2.8)

in which case
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
where
\theta_{|s-t|+1}
represents the autocovariance over lag
|s-t|
.

Example 4
.

Also the multivariate location-scale model can be obtained as a special case of (
2.1
),
by taking
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{I}_{k}
, the
k\times k
identity matrix.
In this case,
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is the unknown location parameter and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is the unstructured covariance matrix
as in Example
2
, with
\bm{\theta}
as in (
2.6
).

Throughout the manuscript we will assume that the parameter
\bm{\theta}
is identifiable in the sense that,

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1})=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{2})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\bm{\theta}_{1}=\bm{\theta}_{2}.

(2.9)

This is true for all models in
Examples
2
,

3
and
4
.
This may not be true in general for the linear mixed effects model
in Example
1
with unknown
\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{G})
and
\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{R})
.
For linear mixed effects models
in (
2.4
),
identifiability of
\bm{\theta}=(\sigma_{0}^{2},\sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}^{2})

holds for particular choices of the design matrices

\mathbf{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{Z}_{r}
.

3
Definitions

We start by representing our observations as points in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
in the following way.
For
r=1,\ldots,k
, let
\mathbf{x}_{r}^{T}
denote the
r
-th row of the
k\times q
matrix
\mathbf{X}
,
so that
\mathbf{x}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.
We represent the pair
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})
as an element in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
defined by

\mathbf{s}^{T}=(\mathbf{y}^{T},\mathbf{x}_{1}^{T},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}^{T})
.
In this way our observations can be represented as
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}
, with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.

3.1
S-estimator

S-estimators are defined by means of a function
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
that satisfies the following properties

(R1)

\rho
is symmetric around zero with
\rho(0)=0
and
\rho
is continuous at zero;

(R2)

There exists a finite constant
c_{0}>0
, such that
\rho
is non-decreasing on
[0,c_{0}]
and constant on
[c_{0},\infty)
;
put
a_{0}=\sup\rho
.

The S-estimator
\bm{\xi}_{n}=(\bm{\beta}_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})
is defined as the solution to the following minimization problem

\begin{split}&\min_{\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta}}\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\\
&\text{subject to}\\
&\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho\left(\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta})}\right)\leq b_{0},\end{split}

(3.1)

where the minimum is taken over all
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
such that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\text{PDS}(k)
,
with
\rho
satisfying (R1)-(R2).

The S-estimator defined by (
3.1
) for the setup in (
2.1
) includes
several specific cases that have been considered in the literature.
The original regression S-estimator introduced by Rousseeuw and Yohai
[
27
]
is obtained
as a special case by
taking
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}
a
1\times q
vector and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\sigma^{2}>0
.
S-estimators for multivariate location and scale, as considered in Davies
[
6
]
and Lopuhaä
[
17
]

can be obtained by taking
\mathbf{X}_{i}
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
as in Example
4
.
For the multivariate regression model in Example
2
,
S-estimators have been considered by Van Aelst and Willems
[
30
]
.
Copt and Victoria-Feser
[
5
]
and Chervoneva and Vishnyakov
[
3
]
consider S-estimators for the parameters in the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
).

The constant
0<b_{0}<a_{0}
in (
3.1
) can be chosen in agreement with an assumed underlying distribution.
For the multivariate regression model in
[
30
]
, it is assumed that

\mathbf{y}_{i}\mid\mathbf{X}_{i}
has an elliptically contoured density of the form

f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})=\text{det}(\mathbf{\Sigma})^{-1/2}h\left((\mathbf{y}-\bm{\mu})^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\mu})\right),

(3.2)

with
\bm{\mu}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
and
h:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
.
For the linear mixed effects model in
[
5
]
, it is assumed that

\mathbf{y}_{i}\mid\mathbf{X}_{i}
has a multivariate normal distribution, which is a special case of (
3.2
)
with
h(t)=(2\pi)^{-k/2}\exp(-t/2)
.
When the underlying distribution corresponds to a density of the form (
3.2
),
then a natural choice is
b_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}}\rho(\|\mathbf{z}\|)
,
where
\mathbf{z}
has density (
3.2
) with
(\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})=(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k})
.
Finally, it should be emphasized that the ratio
b_{0}/a_{0}
determines the breakdown point of the S-estimator
(see Theorem
4
),
as well as its limiting variance (see Corollary
6
).
By choosing the constant
c_{0}
in (R2) one then has to make a trade-off between robustness and efficiency.

Note that at this point we do not assume smoothness of
\rho
or strict monotonicity on
[0,c_{0}]
.
This means that (R1)-(R2) allow the function
\rho(d)=1-\mathds{1}_{[-c_{0},c_{0}]}(d)
, which corresponds to the minimum volume ellipsoid estimator in location-scale models (see
[
26
]
)
and to the least median of squares estimator in linear regression models (see
[
28
]
).
Indeed, with
\rho(d)=1-\mathds{1}_{[-c_{0},c_{0}]}(d)
, the S-estimator
(\bm{\beta}_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})

corresponds to the smallest cylinder

\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c_{0})=\left\{(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}:(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\leq c_{0}^{2}\right\}

(3.3)

that contains at least
n-nb_{0}
points.

Remark 3.1
.

Clearly, the definition of the S-estimator in (
3.1
) has great similarities with
the S-estimator for multivariate location and covariance (see
[
6
]
and
[
17
]
),
defined as the solution
(\mathbf{t}_{n},\mathbf{C}_{n})
to the minimization problem

\begin{split}&\min_{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{C}}\text{\rm det}(\mathbf{C})\\
&\text{subject to}\\
&\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho\left(\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{t})^{T}\mathbf{C}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{t})}\right)\leq b_{0},\end{split}

(3.4)

where the minimum is taken over all
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{C}\in\text{PDS}(k)
.
Even more so, if all
\mathbf{X}_{i}
are assumed to be equal to the same design matrix
\mathbf{X}
of full rank, as was done in
[
5
,
4
]
.
However, there is a subtle, but important difference between minimization problems (
3.4
) and (
3.1
).
The important difference is that in (
3.4
) we minimize over
all
positive definite symmetric
k\times k
matrices

\mathbf{C}
, whereas in (
3.1
), we
only
minimize over positive definite symmetric
k\times k
matrices
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
, which can arise as the image of the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
.
The latter collection is a subset of the other:

\left\{\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathrm{PDS}(k):\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}\right\}\subset\text{PDS}(k),

and will typically be a strictly smaller subset.
This means that the properties of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n})

and
\mathbf{C}_{n}
are related, but the properties of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n})

cannot
simply be derived from
properties of
\mathbf{C}_{n}
,
not even in the case where all
\mathbf{X}_{i}
are equal to the same
\mathbf{X}
.
In fact, this will lead to limiting covariances that differ from the ones found
in
[
5
]
, see Corollary
6
.

3.2
S-functional

The concept of S-functional is needed to investigate local robustness properties of the corresponding S-estimator,
such as the influence function (see Section
8
).
Let
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})
have a probability distribution
P
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
The S-functional
\bm{\xi}(P)=(\bm{\beta}(P),\bm{\theta}(P))

is defined as the solution to the following
minimization problem:

\begin{split}&\min_{\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta}}\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\\
&\text{subject to}\\
&\int\rho\left(\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\leq b_{0},\end{split}

(3.5)

where the minimum is taken over all
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
such that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\text{PDS}(k)
,
with
\rho
satisfying (R1)-(R2).

As a special case, we obtain the S-estimator
\bm{\xi}_{n}=(\bm{\beta}_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})
by taking
P=\mathbb{P}_{n}
,
the empirical measure corresponding to the observations

(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{X}_{n})
.
In view of this connection, existence and consistency of solutions to (
3.1
)
will follow from general results on the existence and the continuity of
solutions to (
3.5
).

The definition of the S-functionals for the multivariate location-scale model given in Lopuhaä
[
17
]

and for the multivariate regression model given by Van Aelst and Willems
[
30
]

can be obtained as special cases of (
3.5
),
by choosing
\mathbf{X}
,
\bm{\beta}
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
as in Examples
4
and
2
,
respectively.
Copt and Victoria-Feser
[
5
]
do not pay attention to S-functionals
or the influence function in the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
).
However, S-functionals for linear mixed effects models can be also be obtained
as a special case of (
3.5
),
by choosing
\mathbf{X}
,
\bm{\beta}
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
as in Example
1
.

4
Existence

We will first establish existence of the S-functional
\bm{\xi}(P)
defined by (
3.5
),
under particular conditions on the probability measure
P
.
As a consequence, this will also yield the existence of the S-estimator, defined by (
3.1
).
Recall that
(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{X}_{n})
are represented as points in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
Note however, that for linear models with intercept the first column of each
\mathbf{X}_{i}
consists of 1’s.
This means that the points
(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
are concentrated in a lower dimensional subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
A similar situation occurs when all
\mathbf{X}_{i}
are equal to the same design matrix, such as in
[
5
]
.
In view of this, define
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{kq}
as the subset with the lowest dimension

p=\text{dim}(\mathcal{X})\leq kq
satisfying

P(\mathbf{X}\in\mathcal{X})=1.

(4.1)

Hence,
P
is then concentrated on the subset
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
of
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
, which
is of dimension
k+p
, which may be of smaller than
k+kq
.

The first condition we require, expresses the fact that
P

cannot have too much mass at infinity, in relation to the ratio
r=b_{0}/a_{0}
.

(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})

There exists a compact set
K_{\epsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
,
such that
P(K_{\epsilon})\geq r+\epsilon
.

The second condition requires that
P
cannot have too much mass at arbitrarily thin strips in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
For
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+kq}
, such that
\|\bm{\alpha}\|=1
,
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta\geq 0
, we define a strip

H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)
as follows:

H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}:\ell-\delta/2\leq\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}\leq\ell+\delta/2\right\}.

(4.2)

Defined in this way, a strip is the area between two parallel hyperplanes
which are symmetric around the hyperplane
H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,0)=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}:\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}=\ell\right\}
.
Since the distance between two parallel hyperplanes

\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}=\ell_{1}
and
\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}=\ell_{2}
is
|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|
,
the strip
H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)
defined as in (
4.2
) has width
\delta
.
We require the following condition

(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})

The value

\delta_{\epsilon}=\inf\left\{\delta:P\left(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)\right)\geq\epsilon,\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+kq},\|\bm{\alpha}\|=1,\ell\in\mathbb{R},\delta\geq 0\right\}

is strictly positive.

According to (
4.1
), in
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
one only needs to consider strips in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.

Both conditions are satisfied for any
0<\epsilon\leq 1-r
by any probability measure
P

that is absolutely continuous.
Clearly, condition
(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})
holds for any
0\leq\epsilon\leq 1-r
for
the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
corresponding to a collection of
n
points

\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
Condition
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
for
\epsilon=(k+p+1)/n

is also satisfied by the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
, when the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is in
general position
, i.e.,
no subset
J\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}
of
k+p+1
points is contained in the same hyperplane
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
Conditions
(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})
and
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
together,
are similar to condition
(\mathrm{C}_{\epsilon})

in
[
17
]
.
The reason that
(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})
slightly deviates from
[
17
]
, is to handle
the presence of
\mathbf{X}
in minimization problem (
3.5
).

Remark 4.1
.

Note that condition
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
is equivalent with

\omega_{\epsilon}=\inf_{P(J)\geq\epsilon}\,\inf_{\|\bm{\alpha}\|=1}\,\inf_{\ell\in\mathbb{R}}\,\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in J}\,|\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}-\ell|>0,

(4.3)

where the infima are taken over all subsets
J\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
with
P(J)\geq\epsilon
,
all vectors
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+kq}
, with
\|\bm{\alpha}\|=1
,
and levels
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
.
Details can be found in
[
16
]
.

To establish existence of the S-functional, we follow the reasoning in
[
17
]
.
The idea is to argue that one can restrict oneself to a compact set for finding solutions to (
3.5
).
When the object function in (
3.5
) is continuous, this immediately yields existence of a solution
of (
3.5
).
To this end, we assume the following condition.

(V1)

The mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is continuous.

The lemma below is fundamental for the existence of the S-functional.
It requires that the identity is in

\mathcal{V}=\{\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\text{PDS}(k):\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}\}

and that
\mathcal{V}
is closed under multiplication with a positive scalar.

(V2)

There exists a
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
, such that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{I}_{k}
.
For any
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathcal{V}
and any
\alpha>0
, it holds that

\alpha\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime})
, for some
\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
.

Conditions (V1)-(V2) are not very restrictive.
For example, all models in Examples
1
to
4
satisfy these conditions.

For any
k\times k
matrix
\mathbf{A}
, let
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{A})\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}
.
We then have the following key lemma for the existence of S-functionals.
The lemma is similar to Lemma 1 in
[
17
]
and its proof can be found in
[
16
]
.

Lemma 1
.

Let
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
0<m_{0}<\infty
,
0<c<\infty
, and
0<\epsilon<1
,
and suppose that the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
satisfies (V2).
Then the following properties hold.

(i)

If
P
satisfies
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
and

P(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c)))\geq\epsilon
,
then
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\geq a_{1}>0
,
where
a_{1}
only depends on
c
and the width
\delta_{\epsilon}
from condition
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
.

(ii)

Suppose
\int\rho(\|\mathbf{y}\|/m_{0})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\leq b_{0}
.
Then for any solution
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})
of (
3.5
),
which is such that
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\geq a_{1}>0
,
it holds that
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\leq a_{2}<\infty
,
where
a_{2}
only depends on
a_{1}
and
m_{0}
.

(iii)

Let
P
satisfy
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})

and suppose that
P(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c))\geq a>0
.
Suppose there exists a compact set
K\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
,
such that
P(K)\geq 1-a+\epsilon
.
If
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\leq a_{2}<\infty
, then

\|\bm{\beta}\|\leq M<\infty
, where
M
only depends on
c
,
a_{2}
,
the set
K
,
and a constant
\gamma_{\epsilon}>0
that can be deduced from condition
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
.

Lemma
1
will ensure that there exists a compact set
that contains all pairs
(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))
that correspond to possible solutions

(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})
of (
3.5
).
To establish that possible solutions
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})
of (
3.5
) are in a compact set,
we need that the pre-image

\{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}:\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in K\}
of a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is again compact.
Recall that subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
are compact if and only if they are closed and bounded,
and note that the pre-image of a continuous mapping of a closed set is closed.
Hence, in view of condition (V1), it suffices to require the following condition.

(V3)

The mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is such that the pre-image of a bounded set is bounded.

Condition (V3) is satisfied by all models in Examples
1
to
4
,
including the linear mixed effects model of Example
1
, as long as the matrix
\mathbf{Z}
is of full rank.
We then have the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

Consider minimization problem (
3.5
) with
\rho
satisfying (R1)-(R2).
Suppose that
P
satisfies
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
, for some
0<\epsilon\leq 1-r
,
where
r=b_{0}/a_{0}
,
and suppose that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3).
Then there exists at least one solution to (
3.5
).

The theorem has a direct corollary for the existence of the S-estimator, when dealing with a collections of points.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}\}
, with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
be a collection of
n
points
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
Define

\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})=\text{maximal number of points of $\mathcal{S}_{n}$ lying on the same hyperplane in~$\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}$.}

(4.4)

For example, if the distribution
P
is absolutely continuous, then

\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})\leq k+p
with probability one.
We then have the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

Consider minimization problem (
3.1
) with

\rho
satisfying (R1)-(R2),
for a collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
,
with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Suppose that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3).
If
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1\leq n(1-r)
, where
r=b_{0}/a_{0}
,
then there exists at least one solution
\bm{\xi}_{n}=(\bm{\beta}_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})

to the minimization problem (
3.1
).

Copt and Victoria-Feser
[
5
]
consider S-estimators
for the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
).
Despite their Proposition 1 about the asymptotic behavior of solutions to their S-minimization problem
[
5
, equation (7)]
,
the actual existence of such a solution is not established.
However, this now follows from our Corollary
1
.
In their case,
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
satisfies conditions (V1) and (V2).
It can be seen, that if all matrices
\mathbf{Z}_{j}
, for
j=1,\ldots,r
, are of full rank, then
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
also satisfies (V3).
The translated bi-weight
\rho
-function proposed in
[
5
]
satisfies (R1)-(R2).
Finally, under their assumption that
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{X}
is the same and
\mathbf{y}_{i}\mid\mathbf{X}\sim N_{k}(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))
,
it follows that
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})\leq k
.
It then follows from Corollary
1
that with
b_{0}\leq a_{0}(n-k-1)/n
,
at least one solution
to their S-minimization problem exists.

For the multivariate regression model from Example
2
, Van Aelst & Willems
[
30
]
do not explicitly
prove existence of the S-estimator.
Since in their case,
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{C}\in\text{PDS}(k)
satisfies (V1)-(V3) and the conditions imposed in
[
30
]

on the
\rho
-function
satisfy (R1)-(R2),
the existence of their S-estimator now also follows from Corollary
1
,
when
b_{0}
is chosen suitably.

Existence of S-estimators is obtained from existence of S-functionals at the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
.
The following corollary shows that existence can be established in general, for probability measures that are close to
P
.
It requires the following condition on
P
.

(C3)

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be the class of all measurable convex subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
Every
C\in\mathfrak{C}
is a
P
-continuity set, i.e.,
P(\partial C)=0
,
where
\partial C
denotes the boundary of
C
.

Corollary 2
.

Suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R1)-(R2) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3).
Let
P_{t}
,
t\geq 0
be a sequence of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
that converges weakly to
P
, as
t\to\infty
.
Suppose that
P
satisfies (C3), as well as
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
, for some
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1-r=b_{0}/a_{0}
.
Then, for
t
sufficiently large, the minimization problem (
3.5
) with
probability measure
P_{t}

has at least one solution
\bm{\xi}(P_{t})
.

Condition (C3) is needed to apply (
A.2
).
Clearly, this condition is satisfied if
P
is absolutely continuous.

5
Continuity and consistency

Consider a sequence
P_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
that converges weakly to
P
, as
t\to\infty
.
By continuity of the S-functional
\bm{\xi}(P)
we mean that
\bm{\xi}(P_{t})\to\bm{\xi}(P)
, as
t\to\infty
.
An example of such a sequence is the sequence of empirical measures
\mathbb{P}_{n}
,
n=1,2,\ldots
, that converges weakly to
P
, almost surely.
Continuity of the S-functional for this sequence would then mean that the S-estimator
\bm{\xi}_{n}
is consistent,
i.e.,
\bm{\xi}_{n}=\bm{\xi}(\mathbb{P}_{n})\to\bm{\xi}(P)
,
almost surely.

We require an additional condition for the function
\rho
.

(R3)

\rho
is continuous and strictly increasing on
[0,c_{0}]
.

For
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})
and
\bm{\xi}=(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})
, define the Mahalanobis distances by

d^{2}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})=d^{2}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}).

(5.1)

We then have the following theorem for the S-functional
\bm{\xi}(P)=(\bm{\beta}(P),\bm{\theta}(P))
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
P_{t}
,
t\geq 0
be a sequence of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
that converges weakly to
P
, as
t\to\infty
,
and let
\bm{\xi}(P_{t})
be a solution to minimization problem (
3.5
) with probability measure
P_{t}
.
Suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R1)-(R3) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3).
Suppose that
P
satisfies (C3), as well as
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
,
for some
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1-r=b_{0}/a_{0}
.
If the solution
\bm{\xi}(P)
of (
3.5
) is unique,
then for any sequence of solutions
\bm{\xi}(P_{t})
,
t\geq 0
, it holds

\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{\xi}(P_{t})=\bm{\xi}(P).

Theorem
2
is an extension of Theorem 3.1 in
[
17
]

on the continuity of S-functionals for multivariate location and scale.
Continuity of S-functionals for multiple regression has been investigated in
[
9
]
.

Continuity of the S-functional will be used to derive the influence function of the S-estimator in Section
8
.
Another nice consequence of the continuity of the S-functional is, that one can directly obtain consistency of the S-estimator.
Consider the S-estimator
\bm{\xi}_{n}
defined by minimization problem (
3.1
).
Recall that
\bm{\xi}_{n}=\bm{\xi}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
, so that we can use Theorem
2
to
establish consistency of the S-estimator.

Corollary 3
.

Let
\bm{\xi}_{n}
be a solution to minimization problem (
3.1
).
Suppose
\rho
satisfies (R1)-(R3)
and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3).
Suppose that
P
satisfies (C3) as well as
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
,
for some
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1-r=b_{0}/a_{0}
.
If the solution
\bm{\xi}(P)
of (
3.5
) is unique,
then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\bm{\xi}_{n}=\bm{\xi}(P),

with probability one.

Theorem
2
and Corollary
3
require that
\bm{\xi}(P)
is
the unique solution to minimization problem (
3.5
).
An example of a distribution
P
for which
\bm{\xi}(P)
is unique,
is when
P
is such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured density (
3.2
).
This situation is very similar to that of multivariate location-scale S-estimators,
for which Davies
[
6
, Theorem 1]
shows that the corresponding S-minimization problem (
3.5
)
has a unique solution.
The next theorem is a direct consequence of that result.
Its proof can be found in
[
16
]
.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose that
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfies (R1)-(R2)
and suppose that the probability distribution
P
of
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})
is such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an
elliptically contoured density
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
from (
3.2
), with
\bm{\mu}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0})
.
Suppose that
h
in (
3.2
) is non-increasing and such that the functions
-\rho
and
h
have at least one common
point of decrease
d_{0}>0
, i.e.,

\rho(s)<\rho(d_{0})<\rho(t)\quad\text{and}\quad h(s)>h(d_{0})>h(t)

for all
s,t\geq 0
, such that
s<d_{0}<t
.
If
\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X}
is non-singular with probability one, then the minimization problem

\begin{split}&\min_{\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta}}\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\\
&\text{subject to}\\
&\int\rho\left(\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})}\right)f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\leq b_{0},\end{split}

(5.2)

where the minimum is taken over all
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
such that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\text{PDS}(k)
,
has the unique solution
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})=(\bm{\beta}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{0})
with probability one.

Minimization problem (
5.2
) seems to be slightly different from the one in (
3.5
).
However, note that when
P
is such that
\mathbf{X}
is equal to a single value with probability one,
both minimization problems are identical.
This situation was considered, e.g., in
[
5
]
.

An elliptically contoured density for
\mathbf{y}_{i}\mid\mathbf{X}_{i}
in the context of S-estimators for specific cases of the model (
2.1
)
has been assumed
in
[
6
]
for the multivariate location-scale model
of Example
4
,
in
[
30
]
for the multivariate regression model
of Example
2
,
and in
[
5
]
for
the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
).
More precisely, in
[
5
]
it is assumed that
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{X}
and that
\mathbf{y}_{i}\mid\mathbf{X}
has a multivariate normal distribution.
In that case, the function
h
in (
3.2
) satisfies all the conditions of Theorem
3
.

6
Global robustness: the breakdown point

Consider a collection of points
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i}),i=1,\ldots,n\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
To emphasize the dependence on the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
denote by
\bm{\xi}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n})=(\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n}),\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n}))
,
the S-estimator,
as defined in (
3.1
).
To investigate the global robustness of S-estimators,
we compute that finite-sample (replacement) breakdown point.
For a given collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
the finite-sample breakdown point
(see Donoho and Huber
[
8
]
)
of a regression S-estimator
\bm{\beta}_{n}
is defined as the smallest proportion of points
from
\mathcal{S}_{n}
that one needs to replace in order to
carry the estimator over all bounds.
More precisely,

\epsilon_{n}^{*}(\bm{\beta}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{n})=\min_{1\leq m\leq n}\left\{\frac{m}{n}:\sup_{\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\left\|\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n})-\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime})\right\|=\infty\right\},

(6.1)

where the minimum runs over all possible collections
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
that can be obtained from
\mathcal{S}_{n}

by replacing
m
points of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
by arbitrary points in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.

The estimator
\bm{\theta}_{n}
determines the covariance estimator
\mathbf{V}_{n}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n})
.
For this reason it seems natural to let the breakdown point of
\bm{\theta}_{n}
correspond to the breakdown of a covariance estimator.
We define the finite sample (replacement) breakdown point of the S-estimator
\bm{\theta}_{n}
at a collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, as

\epsilon_{n}^{*}(\bm{\theta}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{n})=\min_{1\leq m\leq n}\left\{\frac{m}{n}:\sup_{\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\text{dist}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n}))),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))=\infty\right\},

(6.2)

with
\text{dist}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined as

\text{dist}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=\max\left\{\left|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})-\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B})\right|,\left|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{A})^{-1}-\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{B})^{-1}\right|\right\}
,
where the minimum runs over all possible collections
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
that can be obtained from
\mathcal{S}_{n}

by replacing
m
points of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
by arbitrary points in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
So the breakdown point of
\bm{\theta}_{n}
is the smallest proportion of points from
\mathcal{S}_{n}
that one needs to replace in order to
make the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))
arbitrarily large (explosion), or
to make the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))
arbitrarily small (implosion).

Good global robustness is illustrated by a high breakdown point.
The breakdown point of the S-estimators is given the theorem below.
It extends the results for S-estimators of multivariate location and scale,
see
[
6
]
and
[
19
]
, and S-estimators for multivariate regression, see
[
30
]
.
For S-estimators in the linear mixed effects model considered in
[
5
]
, the breakdown point
has not been established.
This will now follow as a special case from the next theorem.
Its proof can be found in
[
16
]
.

Theorem 4
.

Consider the minimization problem (
3.1
) with
\rho
satisfying (R1)-(R2).
Suppose that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3).
Let
\mathcal{S}_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
be a collection of
n
points
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Let
r=b_{0}/a_{0}
and suppose that
0<r\leq(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/(2n)
,
where
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})
is defined by (
4.4
).
Then for any solution
(\bm{\beta}_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})
of minimization problem (
3.5
),

\begin{split}\frac{\lfloor{(n+1)/2}\rfloor}{n}\geq\epsilon_{n}^{*}(\bm{\beta}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{n})&\geq\frac{\lceil{nr}\rceil}{n},\\
\epsilon_{n}^{*}(\bm{\theta}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{n})&=\frac{\lceil{nr}\rceil}{n}.\end{split}

The largest possible value of the breakdown point occurs when
r=(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/(2n)
, in which case

\lceil nr\rceil/n=\lceil(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/2\rceil/n=\lfloor(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1)/2\rfloor/n
.
When the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is in general position, then
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})=k+p
.
In that case the breakdown point of both estimators is at least equal
to
\lfloor(n-k-p+1)/2\rfloor/n
.
When all
\mathbf{X}_{i}
are equal to the same
\mathbf{X}
, in
[
5
,
4
]
,
one has
p=0
and
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})=k
.
In that case, the breakdown point of
\bm{\theta}_{n}
is equal to
\lfloor(n-k+1)/2\rfloor/n
.
This coincides with the maximal breakdown point for affine equivariant estimators
for
k\times k
covariance matrices (see
[
6
, Theorem 6]
).

Remark 6.1
.

Van Aelst & Willems
[
30
]
also take into account the case
r>(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/(2n)
.
For this case, by replacing
\lceil{n-nr}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})
points, a specific solution to the S-minimization problem
is constructed that breaks down.
However, since there may be multiple solutions to the S-minimization problem, this does not necessarily mean that all solutions break down.
In the proof of our Theorem
4
, for the case
r\leq(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/(2n)
, we show that
all solutions to (
3.1
) do not break down, when replacing at most
\lceil nr\rceil-1
points,
and that the covariance part of all solutions do break down, when replacing
\lceil nr\rceil
points.
For the case
r>(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/(2n)
, we can show that all solutions
to (
3.1
) do not break down,
when replacing at most
\lceil{n-nr}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})-1
points.

7
Score equations

Up to this point, properties of S-functionals and S-estimators have been derived from the minimization
problems (
3.1
) and (
3.5
).
To obtain the influence function and to establish the limiting distribution of S-estimators,
we use the score equations that can be found by differentiation of the Lagrangian corresponding to the
constrained minimization problems.
To this end, we require the following additional condition on the function
\rho
,

(R4)

\rho
is continuously differentiable and
u(s)=\rho^{\prime}(s)/s
is continuous,

and the following condition on the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
,

(V4)

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is continuously differentiable.

Obviously, condition (V4) implies the former condition (V1).

7.1
General covariance structures

Let
\bm{\xi}_{P}=(\bm{\beta}_{P},\bm{\theta}_{P})
be a solution to minimization problem (
3.5
).
If we denote the corresponding Lagrange multiplier by
\lambda_{P}
,
then the pair
(\bm{\xi}_{P},\lambda_{P})
is a zero of all partial derivatives

\partial L_{P}/\partial\bm{\beta}
,

\partial L_{P}/\partial\bm{\theta}
,
and
\partial L_{P}/\partial\lambda
,
where
L_{P}
is the Lagrangian given by

L_{P}(\bm{\xi},\lambda)=\log\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))-\lambda\left\{\int\rho\left(\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})-b_{0}\right\}.

If
\mathbb{E}_{P}\|\mathbf{X}\|<\infty
, then under conditions (R4) and (V4), one may interchange the order of integration and differentiation
in
\partial L_{P}/\partial\bm{\beta}
and
\partial L_{P}/\partial\bm{\theta}
, on a neighborhood of
\xi_{P}
.
It follows that besides the constraint in (
3.5
),
the pair
(\bm{\xi}_{P},\lambda_{P})
satisfies

\begin{split}\int u(d)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})&=\mathbf{0}\\
\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})&=0,\end{split}

(7.1)

for
j=1,\ldots,l
,
where
u(s)=\rho^{\prime}(s)/s
and
d=d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})
is defined by (
5.1
),
and where we abbreviate
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
by
\mathbf{V}
.
To solve
\lambda_{P}
from the second set of equations, we multiply the
j
-th equation by
\theta_{j}
and then
sum over
j=1,\ldots,l
.
This leads to

\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\theta_{j}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{l}\theta_{j}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})=0,

which is solved by

\lambda_{P}=\frac{-2\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\theta_{j}(\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\theta_{j})\right)}{\int u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{l}\theta_{j}(\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\theta_{j})\right)\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})}.

When we insert this back into the second equation in (
7.1
), we find

\begin{split}&\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\int u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{l}\theta_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right)\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&\quad-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{l}\theta_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right)\int u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})=0,\end{split}

or briefly

\int u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})=0,\quad j=1,\ldots,l,

(7.2)

where

\mathbf{H}_{j}=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{l}\theta_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right)-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{l}\theta_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right)\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}.

(7.3)

Because
\sum_{j=1}^{l}\theta_{j}\mathbf{H}_{j}=\mathbf{0}
, the system of equations (
7.2
) is linearly dependent.
Similar to
[
17
]
we subtract the S-constraint from each equation.
For each
j=1,\ldots,l
, we subtract the term

\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)(\rho(d)-b_{0})

from the left hand side of equation (
7.2
).
We then find that any solution
\bm{\xi}_{P}
of (
3.5
) satisfies
the following equation

\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})=\mathbf{0},

(7.4)

where
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\bm{\theta}})
, with
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}=(\Psi_{\bm{\theta},1},\ldots,\Psi_{\bm{\theta},l})
, where

\begin{split}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})&=u(d)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})&=u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)(\rho(d)-b_{0}),\end{split}

(7.5)

for
j=1,\ldots,l
, where
\mathbf{H}_{j}
and
d=d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})
are defined in (
7.3
) and (
5.1
),
respectively,
and where we abbreviate
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
by
\mathbf{V}
.

The regression score equation for
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
with the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
for
P
in (
7.4
)
coincides with the one for the regression S-estimator in the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
)
considered in
[
5
]
(see their equation (10)).
The empirical regression score equation also coincides with the one
for the regression S-estimator in the multivariate regression model of Example
2
considered in
[
30
]

(see equation (2.2) in
[
29
]
).
Similarly, the empirical score equation for
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
coincides with the one
for the location S-estimator of Example
4
considered in
[
17
]
.

For general covariance structures the empirical covariance score equation for
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}
does not compare
directly to existing equations in the literature.
However, as we will see in the next subsection,
similar comparisons are available for models with a linear covariance structure.

7.2
Linear covariance structures

In the previous section, we solved
\lambda
from (
7.1
) and subtracted the S-constraint,
leading to score equation (
7.4
) with
\Psi
given in (
7.5
).
The fact that this was done in a specific way has the following reason.
In cases where
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is linear, say

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{j=1}^{l}\theta_{j}\mathbf{L}_{j},

(7.6)

the function
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}
simplifies a lot and can also be related to
the covariance psi-function in
[
17
]
.
Typical models of interest that have a covariance matrix of this type are
the mixed linear effects model from Example
1

and the multivariate regression model from Example
2
.
But also the multivariate location-scale model from Example
4

and the time series model (
2.8
) from Example
3

have linear covariance structures.

When
\mathbf{V}
is of the form (
7.6
), then
\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\theta_{j}=\mathbf{L}_{j}

and
\sum_{j=1}^{l}\theta_{j}(\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\theta_{j})=\mathbf{V}
.
In this case, (
7.3
) simplifies to

\mathbf{H}_{j}=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)\mathbf{V}-k\mathbf{L}_{j}
,
and
\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}
in (
7.5
) becomes

\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)v(d)-ku(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}),

where
u(s)
is defined in (R4) and

v(s)=u(s)s^{2}-\rho(s)+b_{0}.

(7.7)

Using that
\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{B})=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{A})^{T}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{B})
, this can be written as

\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})=-\mathrm{vec}\Big(ku(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}-v(d)\mathbf{V}\Big)^{T}\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right).

On the right hand side we recognize

ku(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}-v(d)\mathbf{V}
,
being the covariance psi-function that also appears in (2.8) in
[
17
]
.
For our purposes we define

\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})=ku(d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}))(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}-v(d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}))\mathbf{V}.

(7.8)

The functions
\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}
, for
j=1,\ldots,l
, can be combined in one expression for the
vector valued function
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}
as follows.
First note that

\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)=\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)

for
j=1,\ldots,l
.
Define the
k^{2}\times l
matrix

\mathbf{L}=\Big[\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{L}_{1}\right)&\cdots&\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{L}_{l}\right)\\
\end{array}\Big].

(7.9)

Then, the column vector
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}=(\Psi_{\bm{\theta},1},\ldots,\Psi_{\bm{\theta},l})
can be written as

\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})=-\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)\mathrm{vec}\left(\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})\right),

where
\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}
is defined in (
7.8
) and
\mathbf{L}
in (
7.9
).
Note that the dependence on
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})
in
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}
is only through the function
\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}
.
We conclude that in the case of a linear covariance structure,
any solution
\bm{\xi}_{P}
of (
3.5
) satisfies (
7.4
),
where
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\bm{\theta}})
, with

\begin{split}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})&=u(d)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})&=-\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)\mathrm{vec}\left(\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})\right)\end{split}

(7.10)

where
d=d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})
is defined in (
5.1
),
and where we abbreviate
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
by
\mathbf{V}
.

For the multivariate regression model in Example
2
,
one has
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{C}
, where

\bm{\theta}=\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{C})
.
The matrix
\mathbf{L}=\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V})/\partial\bm{\theta}^{T}
is then equal to the so-called duplication matrix
\mathcal{D}_{k}
,
which is the unique
k^{2}\times k(k+1)/2
matrix, with the properties

\mathcal{D}_{k}\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{C})=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C})
and
(\mathcal{D}_{k}\mathcal{D}_{k})^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{k}^{T}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C})=\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{C})

(e.g., see
[
20
, Ch. 3, Sec. 8]
).
Because
\mathbf{V}
has full rank, it follows that equation (
7.4
) holds for
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\mathbf{V}})
.
The resulting score equations for the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
corresponding to observations

(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, are then equivalent with the ones found in
[
30
]
.

For the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
),
the covariance matrix
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
has a linear structure with the vector

\bm{\theta}=(\sigma_{0}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}^{2})

of unknown covariance parameters.
The matrix
\mathbf{L}
is then a
k^{2}\times(r+1)
matrix and will typically be of rank
r+1<k^{2}
.
As a consequence, in this case one cannot further simplify equation (
7.4
),
by removing the factor
\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)
from the function
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}
.
The score equation for
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
resulting from the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}

corresponding to observations
(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, is the same as the one
obtained in
[
5
]
.
The corresponding score equation for
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}
differs slightly from the one in
[
5
]
,
because the authors do not subtract a term with
\rho(d)-b_{0}

to remove the linear dependency of the equations (
7.2
).

8
Local robustness: the influence function

For
0<h<1
and
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
fixed, define the perturbed probability measure

P_{h,\mathbf{s}}=(1-h)P+h\delta_{\mathbf{s}},

where
\delta_{\mathbf{s}}
denotes the Dirac measure at
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
The
influence function
of the functional
\bm{\xi}(\cdot)
at probability measure
P
,
is defined as

\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\xi},P)=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{\bm{\xi}((1-h)P+h\delta_{\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\xi}(P)}{h},

(8.1)

if this limit exists.
In contrast to the global robustness measured by the breakdown point,
the influence function measures the local robustness.
It describes the effect of an infinitesimal contamination at a single point
\mathbf{s}
on the functional
(see Hampel
[
11
]
).
Good local robustness is therefore illustrated by a bounded influence function.

8.1
The general case

The theorem below gives the influence function for the S-functional
\bm{\xi}
.
It extends the result for S-functionals of multivariate location and scale
[
17
]
.
Under the assumption that the limit in (
8.1
) exists and
P
has an elliptical contoured density (
3.2
),
Van Aelst and Willems
[
30
]

relate the influence function for S-functionals of multivariate regression
to that of S-functionals of multivariate location and scale.
For the linear mixed effects model considered in
[
5
]
,
the influence function has not been established.
The influence function for these functionals now follows as a special case from the theorem below.

We will show that the limit in (
8.1
) exists and derive its expression at general
P
.
Since the value of
\bm{\theta}
determines the covariance matrix
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
,
we also include the influence function of the covariance functional.
Consider the S-functional at
P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}
.
From the Portmanteau theorem
[
2
, Theorem 2.1]
it can easily be seen that
P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}\to P
, weakly, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Therefore, under the conditions of Corollary
2
and Theorem
2
, it follows
that there exist solutions
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})
and
\bm{\xi}(P)
to minimization problems (
3.5
)
at
P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}
and
P
, respectively, and that
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})\to\bm{\xi}(P)
, as
h\downarrow 0
.

Theorem 5
.

Let
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})
and
\bm{\xi}(P)
be solutions to minimization problems (
3.5
)
at
P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}
and
P
, respectively, and suppose that
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})\to\bm{\xi}(P)
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R4) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4).
Let
\Psi
be defined in (
7.5
) and suppose that

\Lambda(\bm{\xi})=\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),

(8.2)

is continuously differentiable with a non-singular derivative
\mathbf{D}(P)
at
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Then for
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
,

\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\xi},P)=-\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P)).

For the covariance functional
\mathbf{C}(P)=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))
, it holds that

\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}),P)=\left(\frac{\partial\,\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P)))}{\partial\bm{\theta}^{T}}\right)\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\theta},P).

To investigate the local robustness of S-estimators, we derive the following bound
on the influence function for
\bm{\xi}(P)
.

Corollary 4
.

Suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R2) and (R4), and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4).
Then there exist
0<C_{1}<\infty
and
0<C_{2}<\infty
, only depending on
P
, such that
for
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})
it holds that
\|\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\|\leq C_{1}+C_{2}\|\mathbf{X}\|
.

Its proof can be found in
[
16
]
.

8.2
Elliptically contoured densities

When
P
is such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured density (
3.2
)
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is linear,
we can obtain a more detailed expression for the influence function,
This requires the following condition on the function
\rho
,

(R5)

\rho
is twice continuously differentiable,

and the following condition on the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
,

(V5)

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is twice continuously differentiable.

Conditions (R5) and (V5) are needed to establish that
\Lambda
, as defined in (
8.2
),
is continuously differentiable.
Clearly, condition (V5) implies former conditions (V4) and (V1).

Suppose that
P
is such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured density
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
from (
3.2
),
with
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}\in\text{PDS}(k)
.
When the S-functional is affine equivariant,
it suffices to determine the influence function for the case
(\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})=(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k})
.
However, this does not hold in general for the S-functionals in our setting.
The reason is that, for a
k\times k
non-singular matrix
\mathbf{A}
and
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
the matrix

\mathbf{A}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\mathbf{A}^{T}
may not be of the form
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime})
, for some
\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
Examples are the (linear)
covariance structure that corresponds to
the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
)
considered in
[
5
]

or the models discussed in Example
3
.

Nevertheless, note that for the general case with
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}\in\text{PDS}(k)
,
we can still use the fact that, conditionally on
\mathbf{X}
, the distribution of
\mathbf{y}
is the same as that of

\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbf{z}+\bm{\mu}
, where
\mathbf{z}
has a spherical density
f_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}
.
As a consequence, we can still obtain the following result, which
enables one to determine the influence functions of the functionals
\bm{\beta}(P)

and
\bm{\theta}(P)
separately.

If
P
itself is also absolutely continuous, then it satisfies (C3), as well as
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
,
for any
0<\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon\leq 1-r
.
When
\rho
and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy (R1)-(R3) and (V1)-(V3), it follows from Theorem
1
and
Corollary
2

that
\bm{\xi}(P)
and
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
exist, for
h
sufficiently small.
If
h
in (
3.2
) is non-increasing and not constant on
[0,c_{0}^{2}]
, then

\bm{\xi}(P)
is unique, according to Theorem
3
,
so that
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})\to\bm{\xi}(P)
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Hence, in order to apply Theorem
5
, it remains to show that
\Lambda
in (
8.2
)
is continuously differentiable with a non-singular derivative at
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
As a first step we obtain that the derivative of
\Lambda
is a block matrix.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that
P
is
such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured
density
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
from (
3.2
)
and
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}<\infty
.
Suppose that
\bm{\xi}(P)
is a solution to the corresponding minimization problem (
3.5
),
such that
(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}(P),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P)))=(\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})
.
Suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R2), (R4)-(R5) and that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V5) and
has a linear structure (
7.6
).
Let
\Lambda
be defined in (
8.2
) with
\Psi
defined in (
7.10
).
Then

\frac{\partial\Lambda(\bm{\xi}(P))}{\partial\bm{\xi}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\dfrac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}(P))}{\partial\bm{\beta}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\\
\mathbf{0}&\dfrac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\xi}(P))}{\partial\bm{\theta}}\\
\end{array}\right),

where

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}(P))}{\partial\bm{\beta}}=-\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right],

(8.3)

with

\alpha=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)\frac{\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)}{\|\mathbf{z}\|}+\frac{1}{k}\rho^{\prime\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\right],

(8.4)

and

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\xi}(P))}{\partial\bm{\theta}}=\gamma_{1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{L}-\gamma_{2}\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1})\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{L}.

where
\mathbf{L}=\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P)))/\partial\bm{\theta}^{T}
is the
k^{2}\times l
matrix given in (
7.9
)
and

\begin{split}\gamma_{1}&=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{0,\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|^{2}+(k+1)\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]}{k+2}\\
\gamma_{2}&=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{0,\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[2\rho^{\prime\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|^{2}+k\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]}{2k(k+2)},\end{split}

(8.5)

The proof is tedious, but straightforward, and can be found in
[
16
]
.

Remark 8.1
.

The proof of Lemma
2
uses the fact
that

\frac{\partial\Lambda(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\xi}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\xi}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}).

for all
\bm{\xi}
in a neighborhood of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
This holds for general
P
and any covariance structure
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
that satisfies (V2)-(V3) and (V5),
see Lemma
B.3
in
[
16
]
.
Furthermore, Lemma
2
is obtained for a linear covariance structure.
However, with some additional technicalities, this result can also be shown to hold for
\Psi
defined in (
7.5
)
corresponding to general covariance structures.
For general covariance structures one still obtains (
8.3
),
and that

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta},j}(\bm{\xi}(P))}{\partial\theta_{s}}=&-\alpha_{1}\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))}{\partial\theta_{s}}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\right)\\
&+\alpha_{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right)\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right),\end{split}

for
j,s=1,\ldots,l
, and where

\alpha_{1}=\gamma_{1}/k
and
\alpha_{2}=\gamma_{1}/k-\gamma_{2}
,
with
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
from (
8.5
),
and where
\mathbf{H}_{j}
is defined in (
7.3
).

The next corollary gives expressions for the influence functions of the functionals
\bm{\beta}(P)

and
\bm{\theta}(P)
separately, at a distribution
P
that is such that

\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured density.
The proof is tedious, but straightforward, and can be found in
[
16
]
.

Corollary 5
.

Suppose that
P
is such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured
density
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
from (
3.2
),
such that
(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}(P),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P)))=(\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})
.
Let
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})
and
\bm{\xi}(P)
be a solution to minimization problem (
3.5
)
at
P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}
and
P
, respectively, and suppose that
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})\to\bm{\xi}(P)
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Suppose that
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}<\infty
and suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R2)-(R5) and that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V5), and
has a linear structure (
7.6
).
Let
\alpha
,
\gamma_{1}
, and
\gamma_{2}
be defined in (
8.4
) and (
8.5
),
and suppose that
\mathbb{E}_{0,\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\right]>0
.
If
\mathbf{X}
has full rank with probability one, then

\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\beta},P)=\frac{u(d_{0})}{\alpha}\Big(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right]\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}(P))

where
d_{0}^{2}=(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}(P))^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}(P))
and
u(s)=\rho^{\prime}(s)/s
.
If
\gamma_{1}>0
and the
k^{2}\times l
matrix
\mathbf{L}
, as defined in (
7.9
), has full rank,
then
\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\theta},P)
is given by

\begin{split}&\frac{ku(d_{0})}{\gamma_{1}}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1})\mathbf{L})\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}(P))(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}(P))^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\\
&\qquad+\left(-\frac{u(d_{0})d_{0}^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{\rho(d_{0})-b_{0}}{\gamma_{1}-k\gamma_{2}}\right)\bm{\theta}(P).\end{split}

Note that since
\mathbf{L}\bm{\theta}(P)=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P)))=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
,
we can immediately obtain the influence function for the covariance functional
\mathbf{C}(P)=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))
.
From Theorem
5
it immediately follows that
\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}),P)
is given by

\begin{split}&\frac{ku(d_{0})}{\gamma_{1}}\mathbf{L}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1})\mathbf{L})\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta})(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\\
&\qquad+\left(-\frac{u(d_{0})d_{0}^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{\rho(d_{0})-b_{0}}{\gamma_{1}-k\gamma_{2}}\right)\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma}).\end{split}

Since the functions
u(s)s=\rho^{\prime}(s)
,

u(s)s^{2}=\rho^{\prime}(s)s
, and
\rho(s)
are bounded, it follows that

\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\theta},P)
and
\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s},\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}),P)
are bounded uniformly in both
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{X}
,
whereas
\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\beta},P)

is bounded uniformly in
\mathbf{y}
, but not in
\mathbf{X}
.
This illustrates the phenomenon in linear regression that
leverage points can have a high effect on the regression S-estimator.

For the S-estimators in the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
) with normal errors
considered in
[
5
]
, the influence function is not available.
The expression can now be obtained from Corollary
5
.
The expression for
\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\beta},P)
in Corollary
5
coincides with the one found
for the multivariate regression S-functional in
[
30
]
,
where
\alpha>0
is the same constant as the one in the expression
of the influence function for the location S-functional in
[
17
]
.
Furthermore, for the multivariate regression model, one has

\bm{\theta}=\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{C})
and the matrix
\mathbf{L}
is equal to the duplication matrix
\mathcal{D}_{k}
.
From the properties of
\mathcal{D}_{k}
, the expressions for the influence functions simplify.
One finds in this case that

\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\theta},P)=\frac{ku(d)}{\gamma_{1}}\mathrm{vech}\left((\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}(P))(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}(P))^{T}\right)+\left(-\frac{u(d)d^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{\rho(d)-b_{0}}{\gamma_{1}-k\gamma_{2}}\right)\bm{\theta}(P)

and the influence function of the covariance functional
\mathbf{C}(P)=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))
itself
is given by

\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{C},P)=\frac{ku(d)}{\gamma_{1}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}(P))(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}(P))^{T}+\left(-\frac{u(d)d^{2}}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{\rho(d)-b_{0}}{\gamma_{1}-k\gamma_{2}}\right)\mathbf{\Sigma}.

This coincides with the expressions found for the covariance S-functionals
in
[
30
]
and in
[
17
]
.

9
Asymptotic normality

To establish asymptotic normality of the S-estimators, we use the score equations
obtained from differentiation of the Lagrangian corresponding to the minimization problem (
3.1
).
In the same way as before, we obtain score equation (
7.4
), with
P
equal to the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}

corresponding to observations

\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}
, with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
From (
7.4
), we see that any solution
\bm{\xi}_{n}=\bm{\xi}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
to the
S-minimization problem (
3.1
) must satisfy

\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{n})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{n}(\mathbf{s})=\mathbf{0},

(9.1)

where
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\bm{\theta}})
is defined in (
7.5
).

9.1
General case

Writing
\bm{\xi}_{P}=\bm{\xi}(P)
, we decompose (
9.1
) as follows

\begin{split}0=\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{n})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})&+\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})\,\mathrm{d}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(\mathbf{s})\\
&+\int\left(\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{n})-\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})\right)\,\mathrm{d}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(\mathbf{s}).\end{split}

(9.2)

The essential step in establishing asymptotic normality of
\bm{\xi}_{n}
,
is to show that the third term on the right hand side of (
9.2
) is of the order
o_{P}(n^{-1/2})
.
To this end we will apply results from empirical process theory as developed in Pollard
[
23
]
.
This leads to the following theorem.

Theorem 6
.

Suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R1)-(R2) and (R4), such that
u(s)
is of bounded variation,
and suppose that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4).
Let
\bm{\xi}_{n}
and
\bm{\xi}(P)
be solutions to minimization problems (
3.1
)
and (
3.5
), and suppose that
\bm{\xi}_{n}\to\bm{\xi}(P)
in probability.
Suppose that
\Lambda
, as defined in (
8.2
) with
\Psi
defined in (
7.5
),
is continuously differentiable with a non-singular derivative
\mathbf{D}(P)
at
\bm{\xi}(P)

and suppose that
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}<\infty
.
Then
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}(P))
is asymptotically normal with mean zero and
covariance matrix

\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))^{T}\right]\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}.

Theorem
6
is similar to Theorem 4.1 in
[
17
]
.
Note that Theorem
6
confirms the
well know heuristic that relates the limiting covariance of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}(P))

to the influence function of the functional
\bm{\xi}(\cdot)
given in Theorem
5
,

\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))^{T}\right]\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}=\mathbb{E}\left[\text{IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},P)\text{IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},P)^{T}\right].

(9.3)

Van Aelst and Willems
[
30
]
consider the limiting behavior of S-estimators in the multivariate regression model
of Example
2
, but only under
P
for which
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptical contoured density.
Copt and Victoria-Feser
[
5
]
consider asymptotic normality for S-estimators in the
linear mixed effects model (
2.4
) with a constant design matrix
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{X}

and only consider
P
for which
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an multivariate normal distribution.

9.2
Elliptically contoured densities

Consider the special case that
P
is such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured density
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
from (
3.2
),
with
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}\in\text{PDS}(k)
.
As before, in determining the limiting normal distribution of the individual S-estimators,
we cannot use affine equivariance and restrict ourselves to the case
(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k})
.
Instead, we use some of the results obtained in Section
8.2

to establish the limiting normal distributions of the S-estimators
\bm{\beta}_{n}=\bm{\beta}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
,

\bm{\theta}_{n}=\bm{\theta}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
, and
\mathbf{C}_{n}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))
.

Corollary 6
.

Suppose that
P
is such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured
density
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
from (
3.2
),
such that
(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}(P),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P)))=(\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})
.
Let
\bm{\xi}_{n}
and
\bm{\xi}(P)
be solutions to minimization problems (
3.1
)
and (
3.5
), and suppose that
\bm{\xi}_{n}\to\bm{\xi}(P)
in probability.
Suppose that
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}<\infty
and suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R2)-(R5), such that
u(s)
is of bounded variation.
Suppose that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V5), and has a linear structure (
7.6
).
Let
\alpha
,
\gamma_{1}
, and
\gamma_{2}
be defined in (
8.4
) and (
8.5
),
and suppose that
\mathbb{E}_{0,\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\right]>0
.
If
\mathbf{X}
has full rank with probability one, then

\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{n}-\bm{\beta}(P))
is asymptotically normal
with mean zero and covariance matrix

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\right]}{k\alpha^{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right]\right)^{-1}.

If
\gamma_{1}>0
and the
k^{2}\times l
matrix
\mathbf{L}
, as defined in (
7.9
), has full rank,
then
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\theta}_{n}-\bm{\theta}(P))
is asymptotically normal with mean zero and covariance matrix

2\sigma_{1}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}+\sigma_{2}\bm{\theta}(P)\bm{\theta}(P)^{T},

where

\begin{split}\sigma_{1}&=\frac{k(k+2)\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\|\mathbf{z}\|^{4}\right]}{\left(\mathbb{E}_{0,\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|^{2}+(k+1)\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]\right)^{2}}\\
\sigma_{2}&=-\frac{2}{k}\sigma_{1}+\frac{4\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}[\left(\rho(\|\mathbf{z}\|)-b_{0}\right)^{2}]}{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\right]\right)^{2}}\end{split}

Due to the linearity of
\mathbf{V}
,
we can immediately establish asymptotic normality of the covariance
estimator
\mathbf{C}_{n}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n})
.
From Corollary
6
it follows that

\sqrt{n}\left(\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}_{n})-\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\right)=\sqrt{n}\left(\mathbf{L}\bm{\theta}_{n}-\mathbf{L}\bm{\theta}(P)\right)=\mathbf{L}\sqrt{n}\left(\bm{\theta}_{n}-\bm{\theta}(P)\right).

It follows that the limiting covariance of

\sqrt{n}\left(\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n}))-\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\right)
is given by

2\sigma_{1}\mathbf{L}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})^{T}.

Corollary
6
is a direct consequence of Theorem
6
.
Its proof, in particular the derivations of the expressions for the limiting covariances, can be found in
[
16
]
.
Note that the constants

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\right]/(k\alpha^{2})
,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
,
are the same as the ones found in
[
17
]
for the location and covariance S-estimators, respectively.
In fact, Corollary
6
is an extension of Corollary 5.1 in
[
17
]
for S-estimators
in the multivariate location-scale model of Example
4
.

Asymptotic normality of S-estimators in the multivariate regression model of Example
2

follows from Corollary
6
.
These estimators have been considered in
[
30
]
,
but asymptotic normality has not been established.
Under the assumption that the heuristic (
9.3
) holds,
asymptotic relative efficiencies are computed on the basis of this heuristic.
Indeed, now that Corollary
6
has been established,
one may check that (
9.3
) holds.

Finally, note that the limiting covariances of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{n}-\bm{\beta}(P))
and
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\theta}_{n}-\bm{\theta}(P))

in Corollary
6

differ from the ones found in
[
5
]
for the linear mixed effects model (
2.4
)
with
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{X}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
The results in
[
5
]
are obtained by re-parameterizing
\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}=\bm{\mu}
and interpreting
the model as a multivariate location-scale model.
Then building on the results in
[
17
]
for S-estimators of multivariate location-scale,
the limiting covariances in
[
5
]
