are found by application of the delta method.
However, in view of Remark
3.1
this does not seem to be a correct approach.

Remark 9.1
.

Although our expressions for the limiting covariances in Corollary
6

differ from the ones found in Proposition 1 in
[
5
]
, somewhat surprisingly,
they yield the same matrices for the example discussed in Section 5.1 in
[
5
]
.
However, this is a consequence of the specific structure of the design matrices
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Z}
in this example.
One can easily find other design matrices for which the limiting covariances in Corollary
6

yield different matrices as the ones found in
[
5
]
.
Moreover, the corresponding confidence regions based on the expressions in Corollary
6

can be substantially smaller than the ones based on the expressions found in
[
5
]
.
See the simulation in Section
10
.

10
Simulation and data example

We compare the asymptotic results of the S-estimators with their finite sample behavior by means of a simulation.
Moreover we investigate the differences between the expressions found in Corollary
6

and the ones in Copt and Victoria-Feser
[
5
]
.
To this end we will study the behavior of the estimators for samples generated from a model that is close to the one in
[
5
]
:

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}+\gamma_{i}\mathbf{Z}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n,

(10.1)

a linear mixed effects model with
\mathbf{y}_{i}
in dimension
k=4
and all subjects with the same design matrix
\mathbf{X}
for the fixed effects

\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})^{T}
.
Following the setup in
[
5
]
, the matrix
\mathbf{X}
is built as follows.
The first column of
\mathbf{X}
is taken to be a vector
\mathbf{1}
consisting of ones of length four.
The four
x
-values in the second column are generated from a standard normal,
and then
\mathbf{X}
is rescaled to a new matrix
\mathbf{X}=[\mathbf{1}\quad\mathbf{x}]
, such that
\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X}=4\mathbf{I}_{2}
.
For our simulation we used

\mathbf{X}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}1&-0.9504967\\
1&-0.5428346\\
1&1.6650521\\
1&-0.1717207\\
\end{array}\right).

The random effects
\gamma_{i}
are independent
N(0,\sigma_{\gamma}^{2})
distributed random variables, which are independent from the
measurement error
\bm{\epsilon}_{i}\sim N(\mathbf{0},\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\mathbf{R})
.
This leads to a structured covariance
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}^{T}+\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\mathbf{R}
,
with covariance parameter vector
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})^{T}
, where
\theta_{1}=\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}

and
\theta_{2}=\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}
.
Following the setup in
[
5
]
, we set
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=1
and
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}=1
.

In
[
5
]
, the authors took
\mathbf{Z}=(1,1,1,1)^{T}
and
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{I}_{4}
.
With these choices the expression

\text{Var}_{\mathrm{CVF}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\right]}{k\alpha^{2}}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}

(10.2)

found in
[
5
]
for the limiting covariance matrix of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{n}-\bm{\beta})

(see (14) in
[
5
]
),
is equal to our expression

\text{Var}_{\mathrm{LGRG}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\right]}{k\alpha^{2}}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X})^{-1},

(10.3)

found in Corollary
6
,
and similarly for the limiting covariance matrix of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\theta}_{n}-\bm{\theta})
.
However, this is just the consequence of the extreme simple choices for
\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{R}
.
Already, if we keep
\mathbf{X}
as it is, and only take a slight variation of either
\mathbf{Z}
or
\mathbf{R}
, one finds
severe differences
between (
10.2
) and (
10.3
), and similarly for
the expression of the limiting covariance matrix of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\theta}_{n}-\bm{\theta})
.

We considered the following two alternatives

1.

take
\mathbf{Z}=(1,2,3,4)^{T}
and leave
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{I}_{4}
as they are;

2.

take
\mathbf{R}=(1,4,9,16)^{T}
and leave
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Z}=(1,1,1,1)^{T}
as they are.

We generated 10 000 samples of size
n=100
according to model (
10.1
)
and computed the value of S-estimators
\bm{\beta}_{n}
and
\bm{\theta}_{n}

by means of Tukey’s bi-weight

\rho_{\mathrm{B}}(s;c)=\begin{cases}s^{2}/2-s^{4}/(2c^{2})+s^{6}/(6c^{4}),&|s|\leq c\\
c^{2}/6&|s|>c.\end{cases}

(10.4)

and
b_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}[\rho_{B}(\|\mathbf{z}\|;c_{0})]
, with the cut-off value
c_{0}
chosen such that
b_{0}/a_{0}=0.5
.
According to Theorem
4
, this corresponds to (asymptotic) breakdown point 50%.

Figure
1
displays the limiting marginal and joined distributions
of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{n}-\bm{\beta})
in the first row, where we generated the samples with alternative 1.
The histograms and scatterplot correspond to the 10 000 different values of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{n}-\bm{\beta})
.
The dashed curves correspond to the densities and 95% contourlines of the theoretical limiting
marginal and joined normal distributions using the covariance matrix in (
10.2
).
The solid curves correspond to the marginal and joined normal distributions using the covariance matrix in (
10.3
).
The empirical contourlines based on the sample mean and sample covariance of the 10 000 estimates are plotted in dotted lines,
but they almost indistinguishable from the solid contourlines.
We find

\text{Var}_{\mathrm{CVF}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}8.13&1.78\\
1.78&0.72\end{array}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{Var}_{\mathrm{LGRG}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1.97&0.38\\
0.38&0.40\end{array}\right).

Figure 1:
Empirical marginal and joined distributions together with limiting marginal and joined distributions of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{n}-\bm{\beta})
(first row) and

\sqrt{n}(\bm{\theta}_{n}-\bm{\theta})
(second row).

Clearly, the histograms of the repeated estimates for
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
match the graphs of the (marginal)
normal densities with the variances given by
\text{Var}_{\mathrm{LGRG}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})
,
and the scatterplot matches with the 95% contourline corresponding to
\text{Var}_{\mathrm{LGRG}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})
.
Note that the differences with
\text{Var}_{\mathrm{CVF}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})
are quite severe.
For example, this yields that the length of the confidence interval for
\beta_{1}

based on
\text{Var}_{\mathrm{CVF}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})
will be two times larger than the one
based on
\text{Var}_{\mathrm{LGRG}}(\bm{\beta}_{n})
.

The second row in Figure
1
displays the limiting distributions
of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\theta}_{n}-\bm{\theta})
, where we generated the samples with alternative 2.
In
[
5
]
, the limiting covariance matrix was given by (see (15) in
[
5
]
)

(\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathbf{L})^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathbf{L})^{-1}
,
where
\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{I}_{k^{2}}+\mathbf{K}_{k,k})(\mathbf{\Sigma}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma})+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})^{T}
, see Corollary 5.1 in
[
17
]
.
Because

\begin{split}(\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathbf{L})^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}(\mathbf{I}_{k^{2}}+\mathbf{K}_{k,k})&=2(\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathbf{L})^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T},\\
(\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathbf{L})^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})&=\bm{\theta}(P),\end{split}

the expression given in
[
5
]
becomes

\text{Var}_{\mathrm{CVF}}(\bm{\theta}_{n})=2\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathbf{L})^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}\right)\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{L}^{T}\mathbf{L})^{-1}+\sigma_{2}\bm{\theta}(P)\bm{\theta}(P)^{T}.

This differs from our Corollary
6
, which gives

\text{Var}_{\mathrm{LGRG}}(\bm{\theta}_{n})=2\sigma_{1}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}+\sigma_{2}\bm{\theta}(P)\bm{\theta}(P)^{T}.

For the choices of
\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{R}
in
[
5
]
, both expressions are equal.
However, for the alternative choice for
\mathbf{R}
made in alternative 2, one finds

\text{Var}_{\mathrm{CVF}}(\bm{\theta}_{n})=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}20.63&-1.22\\
-1.22&1.77\end{array}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{Var}_{\mathrm{LGRG}}(\bm{\theta}_{n})=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}8.57&-0.82\\
-0.82&0.80\end{array}\right).

Again the differences are quite large.
For example, as a consequence the length of the confidence interval for
\theta_{1}

based on
\text{Var}_{\mathrm{CVF}}(\bm{\theta}_{n})
will be 1.5 times larger than the one
based on
\text{Var}_{\mathrm{LGRG}}(\bm{\theta}_{n})
.

Figure 2:
Left picture: standardized residuals for the S-estimates (horizontal axis)
and the ML estimates (vertical axis).
Right picture: observations for the subjects in the treatment group.

Finally, we illustrate the performance of S-estimators by an application to data from a trial on the treatment of lead-exposed children.
This dataset is discussed in
[
10
]
and consists of four repeated measurements of blood lead
levels obtained at baseline (or week 0), week 1, week 4, and week 6 on 100 children who were randomly assigned to chelation treatment
with succimer (a chelation agent) or placebo.
On the basis of a graphical display of the mean response over time, it is suggested in
[
10
]
that
a quadratic trend over time seems suitable.
We fitted the following model

y_{ij}=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}\delta_{i}+(\beta_{3}+\beta_{4}\delta_{i})t_{j}+(\beta_{5}+\beta_{6}\delta_{i})t_{j}^{2}+\gamma_{1i}+\gamma_{2i}t_{j}+\gamma_{3i}t_{j}^{2}+\epsilon_{ij},

for
i=1,\ldots,100
and
j=1,\ldots,4
, where

(t_{1},\ldots,t_{4})=(0,1,4,6)
refer to the different weeks,

y_{ij}
is the blood lead level (mcg/dL) of subject
i
obtained at time
t_{j}
,
and

\delta_{i}=0
if the
i
-th subject is in the placebo group and
\delta_{i}=1
, otherwise.
The random effects
\bm{\gamma}_{i}=(\gamma_{1i},\gamma_{2i},\gamma_{3i})
,
i=1,\ldots,100
, are assumed to be independent
mean zero normal random vectors with a diagonal covariance matrix consisting of variances
\sigma_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}
,

\sigma_{\gamma_{2}}^{2}
and
\sigma_{\gamma_{3}}^{2}
, respectively.
The measurement errors
\bm{\epsilon}_{i}=(\epsilon_{i1},\ldots,\epsilon_{i4})
,
i=1,\ldots,100
, are assumed to be independent
mean zero random vectors with covariance matrix
\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{4}
,
also being independent of the random effects.
In this way we are fitting a balanced linear mixed effects model with unknown parameters
\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{6})

and
\bm{\theta}=(\sigma_{\gamma_{1}}^{2},\sigma_{\gamma_{2}}^{2},\sigma_{\gamma_{3}}^{2},\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2})
, and a linear covariance structure.

We estimated
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})
by means of maximum likelihood and by means of the S-estimator corresponding to
Tukey’s bi-weight defined in (
10.4
).
The tuning-constant was chosen to be
c=4.097
, which corresponds to asymptotic breakdown point 0.5.
For each estimate
(\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{\bm{\theta}})
, we determined the estimate
\mathbf{V}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}})
for the structured covariance and the
standardized residuals for each subject

\mathrm{RES}_{i}=\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\widehat{\bm{\beta}})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\widehat{\bm{\beta}})}

The residuals for both estimation procedures are visible in the left picture of Figure
2
,
with the residuals determined from the S-estimate on the horizonal axis and
the ones determined from the ML estimate on the vertical axis.
Both estimates identify subject 40 as an outlier, but only the robust S-estimate also clearly identifies observation 98 as outlier.
The extreme large observation in week 6 seems to be the reason that observation 40 is identified as outlier by both methods.
See the right picture in Figure
2
.
Observation 98 also seems to deviate from the overall quadratic trend, by having a suspicious low observation in week 6.
The corresponding S-residual clearly sticks out from the other S-residuals,
whereas this is much less so for the corresponding ML residual.

Appendix A
Proofs and technical lemmas

Proof of Theorem
1

Proof.

Let
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
satisfy the S-constraint in (
3.5
).
Then from (R1)-(R2) it follows that

P(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}),c_{0}))\geq 1-\frac{1}{a_{0}}\int\rho\left(\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})}\right)\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\geq 1-r.

(A.1)

Since
1-r\geq\epsilon
,
Lemma
1
(i) then implies that
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\geq a_{1}>0
.
Because

\lim_{m\to\infty}\int\rho\left(\|\mathbf{y}\|/m\right)\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})=0,

we can find
m_{0}>0
, such that
\int\rho(\|\mathbf{\mathbf{y}}\|/m_{0})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\leq b_{0}
.
Lemma
1
(ii) then yields that
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\leq a_{2}<\infty
.
Application of Lemma
1
(iii), with
a=1-r
together with
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon})
,
implies that
\|\bm{\beta}\|\leq M<\infty
.
It follows that
\bm{\beta}
is in a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is in a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
.

According to (
2.9
), the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is one-to-one,
so that we can restrict
\bm{\theta}
to the pre-image
\mathbf{V}^{-1}(K)
.
Then with conditions (V1) and (V3) it follows that also
\mathbf{V}^{-1}(K)
is compact in
\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
We conclude that for solving minimization problem (
3.5
),
we can restrict ourselves to a compact set
K^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
As
\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))
is a continuous function of

(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})
, due to condition (V1), it must attain a minimum on
K^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{n}
be the empirical measure corresponding to the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
Then
\mathbb{P}_{n}
satisfies
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon})
for any
0<\epsilon\leq 1-r
and
satisfies
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
, for
\epsilon=(\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1)/n
.
Clearly
0<\epsilon\leq 1-r
, where
r=b_{0}/a_{0}
, so according to Theorem
1
there exists at least one solution
to (
3.5
) with
P=\mathbb{P}_{n}
.
This means that there exists at least one solution to (
3.1
).
∎

Proof of Corollary
2

Proof.

First note there exists
0<\eta<\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon
.
According to Ranga Rao
[
24
, Theorem 4.2]
we have

\sup_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}\left|P_{t}(C)-P(C)\right|\to 0,\quad\text{as }t\to\infty.

(A.2)

Because strips
H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)\in\mathfrak{C}
,
property (
A.2
) implies that every strip with
P_{t}(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta))\geq\epsilon+\eta
,
for
t
sufficiently large, must also satisfy

P(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta))\geq\epsilon
.
This means that

\inf\left\{\delta:P_{t}(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta))\geq\epsilon+\eta\right\}\geq\inf\left\{\delta:P(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta))\geq\epsilon\right\}>0.

It follows that, for
t
sufficiently large,
P_{t}
satisfies condition
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon+\eta})
.
Next, consider the compact set
K
from (
\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
).
Without loss of generality we may assume that it belongs to
\mathfrak{C}
.
Therefore,
as
P(K)\geq r+\epsilon^{\prime}
, for
t
sufficiently large
P_{t}(K)\geq r+\epsilon+\eta
.
It follows that, for
t
sufficiently large,
P_{t}
satisfies condition
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon+\eta})
.
Since
\epsilon+\eta<1-r
, according to Theorem
1

at least one solution
\bm{\xi}(P_{t})
exists,
for
t
sufficiently large.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2

Proof.

First note that there exists
0<\eta<\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon
.
Denote
\bm{\xi}(P_{t})=\bm{\xi}_{t}=(\bm{\beta}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t})
.
Similar to (
A.1
) we find that
P_{t}(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t},c_{0}))\geq 1-r
.
Therefore, as
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t},c_{0})\in\mathfrak{C}

and
1-r>1-r-\eta
,
it follows from (
A.2
) that

P(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t},c_{0}))\geq P_{t}(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t},c_{0}))-\sup_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}\left|P_{t}(C)-P(C)\right|\geq 1-r-\eta,

(A.3)

for
t
sufficiently large.
Since
1-r-\eta>\epsilon
, this means that, according to Lemma
1
(i),
there exists
a_{1}>0
only depending on
c_{0}
and
P
, such that
for
t
sufficiently large,

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{t}))\geq a_{1}>0.

Denote
\bm{\xi}(P)=\bm{\xi}_{0}=(\bm{\beta}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{0})
and let
d_{0}(\mathbf{s})=d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\beta}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{0})
.
Then according to Lemma
B.1
in
[
16
]
,
for any
\sigma>-1
,

\int\rho\left(\frac{d_{0}(\mathbf{s})}{1+\sigma}\right)\,\mathrm{d}P_{t}(\mathbf{s})\to\int\rho\left(\frac{d_{0}(\mathbf{s})}{1+\sigma}\right)\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),

as
t\to\infty
.
As the limit is strictly decreasing at
\sigma=0
,
and
\bm{\xi}_{0}
satisfies the constraint in (
3.5
),
we find that for all
\sigma>0
,

\int\rho\left(\frac{d_{0}(\mathbf{s})}{1+\sigma}\right)\,\mathrm{d}P_{t}(\mathbf{s})\leq b_{0},

for
t
sufficiently large.
Hence, similar to the proof of Lemma
1
(ii) we conclude that
for any possible solution
\bm{\xi}_{t}=(\bm{\beta}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t})
,
it must hold that

\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{t}))\leq(1+\sigma)^{2k}\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0})),

for
t
sufficiently large.
As
\sigma>0
can be taken arbitrarily small, we conclude that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{t}))\leq\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0})),

(A.4)

and we find that
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{t}))\leq\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0}))/a_{1}^{k-1}<\infty
,
for
t
large sufficiently large.
Finally, let
K
be the compact set from
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
, so that

P(K)\geq r+\epsilon^{\prime}>r+\epsilon+\eta
.
Then, according to (
A.3
), it
follows from Lemma
1
(iii) with
a=1-r-\eta
, that there exists

0<M<\infty
such that
\|\bm{\beta}_{t}\|\leq M
, for
t
sufficiently large.
This means that there exists a compact set
K^{\prime}
,
such that for
t
sufficiently large the sequence
\{(\bm{\beta}_{t},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{t}))\}\subset K^{\prime}
.
Then, similar to the second part of the proof of Theorem
1
,
the conditions on the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
yield that there exists a compact set
K^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{q+l}
,
such that for
t
sufficiently large, the sequence
\{\bm{\xi}_{t}\}\subset K^{\prime\prime}
.

Consider a convergent subsequence
\{\bm{\xi}_{t_{j}}\}
with
\bm{\xi}_{t_{j}}\to\bm{\xi}_{L}
.
With Lemma
B.1
in
[
16
]
and the fact that
\bm{\xi}_{t_{j}}
satisfies the
S-constraint in (
3.5
) at
P=P_{t_{j}}
,
we find

\int\rho(d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{L}))\,\mathrm{d}P(s)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\int\rho(d(s,\bm{\xi}_{t_{j}}))\,\mathrm{d}P_{t_{j}}(s)\leq b_{0}.

Hence,
\bm{\xi}_{L}
satisfies the S-constraint in (
3.5
),
which has solution
\bm{\xi}_{0}
.
This means that
\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{L}))\geq\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0}))
.
But then from (
A.4
), it follows
\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{L}))=\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0}))
.
Uniqueness of
\bm{\xi}_{0}
together with identifiability (
2.9
) then implies that
\bm{\xi}_{L}=\bm{\xi}_{0}
.
Because
\{\bm{\xi}_{t}\}
eventually stays in a compact set, this means that we must have

\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{\xi}_{t}=\bm{\xi}_{0}
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
3

Proof.

We apply Theorem
2
to the sequence
\mathbb{P}_{n}
,
n=1,2,\ldots
,
of probability measures, where
\mathbb{P}_{n}
is the empirical measure corresponding to

(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{X}_{n})
.
According to the Portmanteau Theorem (e.g., see Theorem 2.1 in
[
2
]
),

\mathbb{P}_{n}
converges weakly to
P
, with probability one.
The corollary then follows from Theorem
2
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5

Proof.

Denote
\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}=\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})
.
This solution satisfies the score equation (
7.4
) for the regression S-functional at
P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}
, that is

\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})\,\mathrm{d}P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}(\mathbf{s})=\mathbf{0}.

We decompose as follows

\begin{split}\mathbf{0}&=\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})\,\mathrm{d}P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}(\mathbf{s})\\
&=(1-h)\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})+h\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})\\
&=(1-h)\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})+h\Big(\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})-\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P))\Big)+h\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P)).\end{split}

We first determine the order of
\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P)
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Because
\bm{\xi}\mapsto\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi})
is continuous, it follows that

\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})=\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P))+o(1),\qquad\text{as }h\downarrow 0.

Furthermore, because
\bm{\xi}\mapsto\Lambda(\bm{\xi})
is continuously differentiable at
\bm{\xi}(P)
, we have that

\begin{split}\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})&=\Lambda(\bm{\xi}(P))+\mathbf{D}(P)(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P))+o(\|\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P)\|).\end{split}

Since
\bm{\xi}(P)
is the S-functional at
P
, it is a zero of the corresponding score equation, i.e.,

\Lambda(\bm{\xi}(P))=0
.
It follows that

\mathbf{0}=(1-h)\mathbf{D}(P)(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P))+o(\|\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P)\|)+o(h)+h\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P)).

Because
\mathbf{D}(P)
is non-singular
and
\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P))
is fixed, this implies

\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P)=O(h)
.
After inserting this in the previous equality, it follows that

\begin{split}\mathbf{0}&=(1-h)\mathbf{D}(P)(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P))+h\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P))+o(h)\\
&=\mathbf{D}(P)(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P))+h\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P))+o(h).\end{split}

We conclude

\frac{\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}-\bm{\xi}(P)}{h}=-\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P))+o(1),\qquad\text{as }h\downarrow 0.

This means that the limit of the left hand side exists and

\text{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\xi},P)=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{\bm{\xi}((1-h)P+h\delta_{\mathbf{s}_{0}})-\bm{\xi}(P)}{h}=-\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\xi}(P)).

Next, consider the covariance functional
\mathbf{C}(P)=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))
.
By definition

\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}),P)=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}))-\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}(P))}{h}.

Due to (V4), by applying the chain rule
(e.g., see
[
20
, Theorem 12, page 108]
), we find

\begin{split}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}))-\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C}(P))&=\frac{\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P)))}{\partial\bm{\theta}^{T}}\Big(\bm{\theta}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})-\bm{\theta}(P)\Big)+o(\|\bm{\theta}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})-\bm{\theta}(P)\|)\\
&=\left(\frac{\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P)))}{\partial\bm{\theta}^{T}}\right)\Big(\bm{\theta}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})-\bm{\theta}(P)\Big)+o(h).\end{split}

Dividing by
h
and letting
h\downarrow 0
, finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6

Proof.

Write
\bm{\xi}_{P}=\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Then from (
9.2
) and Lemma
B.8
in
[
16
]
, it follows that

\mathbf{0}=\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{n})+\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})\,\mathrm{d}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(\mathbf{s})+o_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

(A.5)

From Theorem
2
, we know that
\bm{\xi}_{n}\to\bm{\xi}_{P}
with probability one.
For the first term on the right hand side we have that

\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{n})=\mathbf{D}(P)(\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P})+o_{P}(\|\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P}\|)=\mathbf{D}(P)(\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P})+o_{P}(\|\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P}\|),

using that
\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P})=\mathbf{0}
, due to the fact that
\bm{\xi}_{P}
is the solution of (
3.5
),
see also (
7.4
).
From Lemma
B.2
in
[
16
]
we have that
\|\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}\|\leq C_{1}\|\mathbf{X}\|

and
\|\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}\|\leq C_{2}
, for universal constants
0<C_{1},C_{2}<\infty
.
Hence, from the conditions of the theorem, it follows that

\mathbb{E}\|\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\|^{2}<\infty
.
This means that for the second term on the right hand side of (
A.5
),

\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})\,\mathrm{d}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)(\mathbf{s})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{P})-\mathbb{E}\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})\right)=O_{P}(n^{-1/2}),

according to the central limit theorem.
It follows that

\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{D}(P)(\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P})+o_{P}(\|\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P}\|)+O_{P}(n^{-1/2}),

so that
\|\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P}\|=O_{P}(n^{-1/2})
.
If we insert this is in (
A.5
), we obtain

\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{D}(P)(\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{P})-\mathbb{E}\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})\right)+o_{P}(n^{-1/2}),

from which it follows that

\sqrt{n}(\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P})=-\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Psi(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{P})-\mathbb{E}\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})\right)\right)+o_{P}(1).

After application of the central limit theorem, we conclude that
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}_{P})
is asymptotically normal with mean zero and covariance matrix

\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})^{T}\right]\mathbf{D}(P)^{-1},

where we use that
\mathbb{E}\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})=\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P})=\mathbf{0}
.
This proves the theorem.
∎

Appendix B
Supplemental Material

For later use we first define two important matrix norms and mention some useful properties.
For
m\times n
real-valued matrices
\mathbf{A}
, we define the Euclidean norm or Frobenius norm
as

\|\mathbf{A}\|=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij}^{2}}.

and the spectral norm by

\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}=\sup_{\|\mathbf{u}\|=1}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}\|.

Recall that for real-valued
\mathbf{A}
, the largest eigenvalue is defined by

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})=\sup_{\|\mathbf{u}\|=1}\mathbf{u}^{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}.

(B.1)

This means that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}^{2}=\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})
.
Other useful properties are

\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|\leq\sqrt{\min(m,n)}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}

(B.2)

and

\|\mathbf{A}\|^{2}=\text{tr}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})=\text{tr}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{T}),

(B.3)

and

\|\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^{T}\|=\|\mathbf{u}\|\|\mathbf{v}\|

(B.4)

for
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
When
\mathbf{A}
is symmetric then
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})=\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{2})=\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})^{2}
.
In that case

\left|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})\right|=\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|\leq\sqrt{\min(m,n)}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}=\sqrt{\min(m,n)}\left|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})\right|.

(B.5)

Finally, note that both matrix norms are submultiplicative, that is

\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{B}\|_{2},

(B.6)

and

\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\|\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|\|\mathbf{B}\|.

(B.7)

B.1
Proofs of Section
4

Proof of Lemma
1

Proof.

Note that cylinder
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c)
is contained
in some strip
H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,2c\sqrt{\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))})
. It then follows from
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
that

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\geq\delta^{2}_{\epsilon}/4c^{2}
.
This proves (i).
Let
\bm{\theta}_{0}
be such that the pair
(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0}))=(\mathbf{0},m_{0}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k})

satisfies the S-constraint in (
3.5
),
which is possible due to condition (V2).
It then follows that for any solution

(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))
of (
3.5
), one must have

\text{det}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\leq m_{0}^{2k}
.
The lower bound on the smallest eigenvalue then implies

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\leq m_{0}^{2k}/a_{1}^{k-1}<\infty
,
which proves (ii).
Note that

\gamma_{\epsilon}=\inf_{P(J)\geq\epsilon}\inf_{\|\bm{\gamma}\|=1}\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in J}\|\mathbf{X}\bm{\gamma}\|>0,

(B.8)

where the infima are taken over all subsets
J\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
with
P(J)\geq\epsilon

and all unit vectors
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.
This can be seen as follows.
Take

\bm{\alpha}^{T}=(\mathbf{0}^{T},\bm{\gamma}^{T},\ldots,\bm{\gamma}^{T})/k
,
where
\mathbf{0}
is a
k
-vector of zeros, so
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
and
\|\bm{\alpha}\|=1
.
Then, with
\ell=0
,
we have

\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}-\ell=(\bm{\gamma}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{1}+\cdots+\bm{\gamma}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{k})/k
.
Note that
\|\mathbf{X}\bm{\gamma}\|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\mathbf{x}_{j}^{T}\bm{\gamma})^{2}
.
Therefore, if
\gamma_{\epsilon}=0
, then
\bm{\gamma}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{j}=0
, for all
j=1,\ldots,k
,
which means that
\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}-\ell=0
.
This would be in contradiction with (
4.3
),
which is equivalent to
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
, according to Remark
4.1
.
Now, note that

P(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c)\cap K)\geq P(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c))-P(K^{c})\geq a-1+1-a+\epsilon=\epsilon.

Hence,
according to (
B.8
), there exists

\mathbf{s}_{0}=(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{X}_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c)\cap K
,
such that
\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\gamma}\|\geq\gamma_{\epsilon}>0
, for all
\bm{\gamma}
with
\|\bm{\gamma}\|=1
.
Because
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c)
, it holds

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}\|^{2}&\leq(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta})\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\leq c\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\leq ca_{2},\end{split}

due to part (ii).
Because
\mathbf{s}_{0}=(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{X}_{0})\in K
, this means that

\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}\|\leq\|\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}\|+\|\mathbf{y}_{0}\|\leq\sqrt{ca_{2}}+\sup_{(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in K}\|\mathbf{y}\|.

Because
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta},c)
,
together with (
B.8
) we conclude that

\|\bm{\beta}\|=\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}\|\times\frac{\|\bm{\beta}\|}{\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}\|}\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{\epsilon}}\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}\|\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{\epsilon}}\left(\sqrt{ca_{2}}+\sup_{(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in K}\|\mathbf{y}\|\right)<\infty.

This proves part (iii).
∎

Proof of Remark
4.1

Proof.

Suppose that
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
holds and suppose that
\omega_{\epsilon}=0
.
Then there exists a sequence
(J_{n},\bm{\alpha}_{n},\ell_{n})
,
with
J_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
,
\bm{\alpha}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+kq}
,
\|\bm{\alpha}_{n}\|=1
, and
\ell_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
,
such that
P(J_{n})\geq\epsilon
, for all
n=1,2,\ldots
,
and

\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in J_{n}}|\bm{\alpha}_{n}^{T}\mathbf{s}-\ell_{n}|\to 0.

This means that for
n
sufficiently large

\delta_{n}=2\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in J_{n}}|\bm{\alpha}_{n}^{T}\mathbf{s}-\ell_{n}|<\delta_{\epsilon}
.
Then, there exists a strip
H(\bm{\alpha}_{n},\ell_{n},\delta_{n})

containing
J_{n}
, such that
\delta_{n}<\delta_{\epsilon}

and

P(H(\bm{\alpha}_{n},\ell_{n},\delta_{n})\cap(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}))\geq P(J_{n})\geq\epsilon
.
This would be in contradiction with the definition of
\delta_{\epsilon}
in
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
.
On the other hand, suppose that (
4.3
) holds and suppose that
\delta_{\epsilon}=0
.
That means that we can find a sequence
(\bm{\alpha}_{n},\ell_{n},\delta_{n})
,
with
\bm{\alpha}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
,
\|\bm{\alpha}_{n}\|=1
, and
\ell_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0

and
P(H(\bm{\alpha}_{n},\ell_{n},\delta_{n})\cap(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}))\geq\epsilon
, for all
n=1,2,\ldots
.
This means that for
n
sufficiently large
\delta_{n}<2\omega_{\epsilon}

and then

\sup_{\mathbf{s}\in H(\bm{\alpha}_{n},\ell_{n},\delta_{n})\cap\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}}|\bm{\alpha}_{n}^{T}\mathbf{s}-\ell_{n}|=\delta_{n}/2<\omega_{\epsilon},

which would contradict definition (
4.3
).
∎

Lemma B.1
.

Let
P_{t}
,
t\geq 0
be a sequence of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
that converges weakly to
P
, as
t\to\infty
.
Let
\bm{\xi}_{t}=(\bm{\beta}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t})
,
t\geq 0
, be a sequence in
\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
such that
\bm{\xi}_{t}\to\bm{\xi}_{L}
, as
t\to\infty
.
If
g(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})=\rho(d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})/\alpha)
, for some
\alpha>0
fixed, where

\rho
satisfies (R2)-(R3) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1),
then

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int g(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}P_{t}(\mathbf{s})=\int g(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{L})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}).

Proof.

Let
g_{t}(\mathbf{s})=g(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{t})
and
g_{L}(\mathbf{s})=g(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{L})
.
Then for every sequence
\{\mathbf{s}_{t}\}
, such that
\mathbf{s}_{t}\to\mathbf{s}
, we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}g_{t}(\mathbf{s}_{t})=g_{L}(\mathbf{s}).

Now, apply Theorem 5.5 from
[
2
]
.
Let
\Gamma:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be the function

\Gamma(u)=u1_{[0,a_{0}]}(u)+a_{0}1_{(a_{0},\infty)}(u),

which is bounded and uniformly continuous.
Then as a consequence of
P_{t}\to P
weakly, we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int g(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}P_{t}(\mathbf{s})=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int\Gamma(g_{t}(\mathbf{s}))\,\mathrm{d}P_{t}(\mathbf{s})=\int\Gamma(g_{L}(\mathbf{s}))\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})=\int g(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{L})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}).

∎

B.2
Proofs of Section
5

Proof of Theorem
3

Proof.

Davies
[
6
]
defines location-scale S-estimators by means of a function
\kappa:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
.
It relates to our
\rho
-function as
\rho(d)=a_{0}(1-\kappa(d^{2}))
.
The S-minimization problem considered in
[
6
]
can be formulated in our notation
as follows

\begin{split}&\min_{\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{A}}\text{det}(\mathbf{A})\\
&\text{subject to}\\
&\int\rho\left(\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\alpha})^{T}\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\alpha})}\right)f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\leq b_{0},\end{split}

(B.9)

where the minimum is taken over all
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathrm{PDS}(k)
.
The conditions (R1)-(R2) imply the conditions on
\kappa(s)=1-\rho(\sqrt{s})/a_{0}
imposed in
[
6
]
,
and
\kappa
and
h
have a common point of decrease.
It then follows from
Theorem 1 in
[
6
]
that (
B.9
) has a unique solution

(\bm{\alpha}^{*},\mathbf{A}^{*})=(\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})=(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0})).

Since this solution is unique, candidate solutions to (
B.9
)
must be of the form
(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{A})=(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))
,
for some
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
It follows that minimization problem (
B.9
) is
equivalent to minimization problem (
5.2
).
As a consequence, minimization problem (
5.2
) has a unique solution
(\bm{\beta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{*})

for which
\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{*})=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0})
.
Since
\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X}
is non-singular we can multiply
\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}
from the left by
(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{T}
.
It then follows that
\bm{\beta}^{*}=\bm{\beta}_{0}
.
Finally, from (
2.9
) we find that
\bm{\theta}^{*}=\bm{\theta}_{0}
.
This proves the theorem.
∎

B.3
Proofs for Section
6

Proof of Theorem
4

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may assume that
c_{0}=1
and
\sup\rho=1
, so that
r=b_{0}
.
The first step is to show that for both estimators

\epsilon^{*}_{n}\geq\lceil nr\rceil/n
.
To this end, consider a collection
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
obtained from the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
by replacing
at most
m=\lceil nr\rceil-1
number of points in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
We must show that at least one solution
(\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}),\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}))

to the S-minimization problem (
3.1
)
exists for the corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
, and that
all possible solutions do not break down.

Denote a possible solution to the S-minimization problem (
3.1
) corresponding to the corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
,
by

\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime}=(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m})=(\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}),\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m})),

and consider the corresponding cylinder

\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m},1)
defined by (
3.3
).
We apply Lemma
1
to the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{m}^{\prime}
corresponding to the
corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
of
n
points.
Because
\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime}=(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime})

must satisfy the S-constraint in (
3.1
),
one can argue as in (
A.1
),

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{m}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m},1))&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in S_{m}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m},1)\right\}\\
&\geq 1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in S_{m}^{\prime}}\rho\left(d(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime})\right)\geq 1-b_{0},\end{split}

where
d(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime})
is defined in (
5.1
).
It follows that the cylinder

\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m},1)

must contain at least
\lceil n-nb_{0}\rceil=\lceil n-nr\rceil
number of points from the corrupted collection

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
.
Furthermore, since
r\leq(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/(2n)
,
for any such subset of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
it holds that
it contains

\lceil n-nr\rceil-m=n-\lfloor{nr}\rfloor-\lceil nr\rceil+1\geq\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1

(B.10)

points of the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
It follows that the measure
\mathbb{P}_{m}^{\prime}
satisfies condition
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
,
for
\epsilon=(\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1)/n
and
with the value
\delta_{\epsilon}>0
only depending on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
Moreover, we also have that
\mathbb{P}_{m}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m},1))\geq\epsilon
,
for
\epsilon=(\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1)/n>0
.
According to Lemma
1
(i), it then follows that

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m}))\geq a_{1}>0
,
where
a_{1}
only depends on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

Because
nb_{0}-m=nr-\lceil nr\rceil+1>0
and

\lim_{R\to\infty}\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\rho\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|}{R}\right)=0,

we can find an
R_{0}>0
, only depending on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
such that

\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\rho\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|}{R_{0}}\right)\leq nb_{0}-m.

The collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
contains
n-m
points of the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
Consider the smallest
M>0
, such that

\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}}\rho\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|}{M}\right)\leq nb_{0}-m.

Because
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}
has less points than
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
it holds that
M\leq R_{0}
.
It follows that

\begin{split}\int\rho\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{y}\|}{R_{0}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{m}^{\prime}(\mathbf{s})&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\rho\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|}{R_{0}}\right)\\
&\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in S_{m}^{\prime}}\rho\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|}{M}\right)\\
&\leq\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in S_{m}^{\prime}\cap S_{n}}\rho\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|}{M}\right)+m\right)\leq b_{0}.\end{split}

According to Lemma
1
(ii), it then follows that

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime}))\leq a_{2}<\infty
.
where
a_{2}
only depends on
a_{1}
and the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

To show that the estimate
\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime}
stays bounded, recall that
the cylinder
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m},1)
contains a subset
J_{0}
of

\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1
points from the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
according to (
B.10
).
By definition,
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1
original points cannot be on the same hyperplane, so that

\gamma_{n}=\inf_{J\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}}\inf_{\|\bm{\gamma}\|=1}\max_{\mathbf{s}\in J}\|\mathbf{X}\bm{\gamma}\|>0.

where the first infimum runs over all subsets
J\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}
of
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1
points.
By definition of
\gamma_{n}
,
there exists an original point
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in J_{0}\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}\cap\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m},1)
, such that

\|\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m}\|=\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m}\|\times\frac{\|\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m}\|}{\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m}\|}\leq\frac{1}{\gamma_{n}}\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m}\|.

Because
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m},1)
,
similar to the proof of Lemma
1
(iii), it follows
that
\|\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m}\|^{2}\leq a_{2}
,
and because
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, we have that

\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m}\|\leq\sqrt{a_{2}}+\max_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|<\infty.

We conclude that there exists a compact set
K_{n}
, only depending on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
that contains the pair
(\bm{\beta}^{\prime}_{m},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}_{m}))
.
Similar to the reasoning in the proof of Theorem
1
,
it follows that at least one solution
(\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}),\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}))

to the S-minimization problem (
3.1
)
exists for the collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
, and that
all possible solutions
\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m})
and
\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m})
do not break down.

We continue by showing

\epsilon^{*}_{n}(\bm{\theta}_{n})\leq\lceil nr\rceil/n
.
Replace
m=\lceil nr\rceil
points of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
to obtain a corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}

of
n
points.
Suppose that a solution
\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime}=(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime})=(\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}),\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}))

exists to the S-minimization problem (
3.1
)
corresponding to the corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
.
We must show that the estimate
\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime}
breaks down.
Note that the estimates
\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime}
and
\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime}
satisfy the S-constraint
in (
3.1
) for the corrupted collection,

\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\rho\left(d(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime})\right)\leq nr.

(B.11)

If all
m=\lceil nr\rceil
replaced points are outside the cylinder
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime},1)
, then

\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\rho\left(d(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime})\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}}\rho\left(d(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime})\right)+\lceil nr\rceil>nr,

when
nr\notin\mathds{N}
.
When
nr\in\mathds{N}
, then by assumption
n-m=n-\lceil nr\rceil\geq\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1
.
Hence, there is at least one point
\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
for which
d(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime})>0
.
Because
\rho
is strictly increasing on
[0,1]
, this implies

\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\rho\left(d(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime})\right)>nr.

We conclude that at least one replaced point
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{i})\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime},1)
.
Similarly, if all original points in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
are outside
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime},1)
, then

\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\rho\left(d(\mathbf{s}_{i},\bm{\xi}_{m}^{\prime})\right)\geq n-\lceil nr\rceil\geq n-\lceil n-nr\rceil+\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})>nr,

which is in contradiction with (
B.11
).
Therefore, the cylinder
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime},1)

must contain a point
\mathbf{s}_{0}=(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{X}_{0})
from the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
as well as one replaced point
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{i})
.

Note that for each point
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime},\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime},1)
it holds that

\frac{|\mathbf{q}_{1}^{T}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime})|^{2}}{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime}))}+\cdots+\frac{|\mathbf{q}_{k}^{T}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{m}^{\prime})|^{2}}{\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime}))}\leq 1,

(B.12)

where
\lambda_{j}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime}))>0
, for
j=1,\ldots,k
, are the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime})

and
\mathbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{q}_{k}
are the corresponding orthonormal eigenvectors.
Now, replace all
m
points by
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{0})
,
where
\mathbf{0}
is a
k\times q
matrix of zeros and

\mathbf{z}=t\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{q}_{j}
, so that
\mathbf{q}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{z}=t
, for each
j=1,\ldots,k
.
By sending
t\to\infty
and the fact that at least one replaced point
(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{0})

satisfies (
B.12
), it follows that

\lambda_{j}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime}))\to\infty
, for each
j=1,\ldots,k
.
This means
\bm{\theta}_{m}^{\prime}
breaks down.

The upper bound for
\epsilon_{n}^{*}(\bm{\beta}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{n})
follows from the fact that

\bm{\beta}_{n}
is regression equivariant.
Similar to Theorem 2 in
[
19
]
it can be shown that
the maximal breakdown point of regression equivariant estimators is
\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor/n
.
This proves the theorem.
∎

B.4
Proofs for Section
8

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R2), (R4) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4).
Let
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\bm{\theta}})
, as defined in (
7.5
).
Then there exist
0<C_{1}<\infty
and
0<C_{2}<\infty
, only depending on
P
, such that

\|\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\|\leq C_{1}\|\mathbf{X}\|

and
\|\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\|\leq C_{2}
.

Proof.

Consider the expression of
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
in (
7.5
).
Consecutively, we apply (
B.3
),
(
B.1
),
(
B.5
),
(
B.6
),
and
(
B.2
).
This gives

\begin{split}\left\|\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\right\|^{2}&=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\leq\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1/2})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&=d^{2}\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq d^{2}\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{T}\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq d^{2}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1}),\end{split}

(B.13)

where

d=d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})
as defined by (
5.1
),
and where we abbreviate
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
by
\mathbf{V}
.
This means that

\|\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\|\leq|u(d)d|\times\|\mathbf{X}\|\times\sqrt{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))^{-1})}.

From (R2) and (R4) it follows that
u(s)s=\rho^{\prime}(s)
is bounded.
This means that there exists a universal constant
0<C_{1}<\infty
, such that

\|\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\|\leq C_{1}\|\mathbf{X}\|.

For
\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}=(\Psi_{\bm{\theta},1},\cdots,\Psi_{\bm{\theta},l})
, we have

\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})=u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)(\rho(d)-b_{0}),

for
j=1,\ldots,l
,
where we write
\mathbf{V}
instead of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
.
Recall that

\mathbf{H}_{j}=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{l}\theta_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right)-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{l}\theta_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right)\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}.

To bound
\mathbf{H}_{j}
, we first obtain a bound on

(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}).

(B.14)

Note that
\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\theta_{j}
is symmetric, but not necessarily positive definite.
When (
B.14
) is positive, then
application of (
B.1
) gives

\begin{split}0<(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})&\leq d^{2}\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right)\\
&\leq d^{2}\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right\|^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|\\
&\leq d^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1}),\end{split}

according to (
B.7
) and (
B.5
).
When (
B.14
) is negative, then similarly

\begin{split}0<(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\left(-\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})&\leq d^{2}\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\left(-\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right)\\
&\leq d^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1}).\end{split}

It follows that

\left|(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\right|\leq d^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1}).

(B.15)

Furthermore, according to (V4),
the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is continuously differentiable.
This means that
there exists a universal constant
0<M_{1}<\infty
, such that

\max_{1\leq j\leq l}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|\leq M_{1}.

(B.16)

Because
\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\theta_{j}
is symmetric, according to (
B.5
)
and (
B.6
)

\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\right|\leq\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right\|_{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{k}\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|.

Together with (
B.16
),
we find

\left|\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\right|\leq k\left|\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\right|\leq k^{3/2}M_{1}\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right),

(B.17)

where we abbreviate
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))
by
\mathbf{V}
.
It then follows there exists a constant
0<M_{2}<\infty
,
only depending on
P
, such that at
\bm{\theta}(P)
,

\max_{1\leq j\leq l}\left|(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\right|\leq d^{2}M_{2}.

(B.18)

From (R2) and (R4) it follows that
u(s)s^{2}=\rho^{\prime}(s)s
and
\rho(s)-b_{0}
are bounded.
Together with (
B.17
),
it follows
that
there exists a universal constant
0<C_{2}<\infty
, such that

\|\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\|\leq C_{2},

for all
j=1,\ldots,l
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4

Proof.

Take
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})
fixed and consider
\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\xi},P)
.
Since
\mathbf{D}(P)
does not depend on
\mathbf{s}
, from Theorem
5
and Lemma
B.2
,
it follows immediately that
\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\xi},P)
remains bounded in
\mathbf{y}
,
but not necessarily in
\mathbf{X}
.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Consider
\Lambda
as defined by (
8.2
) with
\Psi
defined in (
7.5
).
Suppose that
\rho
satisfies (R2) and (R5) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V5).
Furthermore, suppose that
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}<\infty
.
Let
\bm{\xi}(P)
be a solution to (
3.5
) and let
N
be an open neighborhood of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Then,

\Lambda
is continuous differentiable at
\bm{\xi}(P)
and
for all
\bm{\xi}\in N
,

\frac{\partial\Lambda(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\xi}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\xi}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}).

Proof.

Write
\partial\Lambda/\partial\bm{\xi}
as the block matrix

\frac{\partial\Lambda(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\xi}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\dfrac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}&\dfrac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}\\
\\
\dfrac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}&\dfrac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}\\
\end{array}\right),

(B.19)

where

\begin{split}\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi})&=\int\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),\\
\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\xi})&=\int\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}).\end{split}

We prove the lemma for each block separately.
Consider
\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}/\partial\bm{\beta}
.
We have

\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}=-\frac{u^{\prime}(d)}{d}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}-u(d)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X},

(B.20)

where
d=d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})
is defined by (
5.1
)
and where we abbreviate
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
by
\mathbf{V}
.
First note that, according to (R5),
\bm{\xi}\mapsto u^{\prime}(d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}))/d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
, for each
\mathbf{s}
fixed such that
d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\neq 0
.
Together with (V1), this means that for such
\mathbf{s}
fixed,
\bm{\xi}\mapsto\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})/\partial\bm{\beta}
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
For the first term on the right hand side of (
B.20
),
we apply (
B.4
) and (
B.13
).
This gives

\left\|\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right\|=\left\|\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\right\|^{2}\leq d^{2}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1}).

Similarly, for the second term on the right hand side of (
B.20
),
after application of (
B.7
) and (
B.5
), we get

\left\|\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right\|\leq\left\|\mathbf{X}\right\|^{2}\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right\|\leq\sqrt{q}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1}).

Since
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})
is bounded uniformly on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
and
because
u(s)
and
u^{\prime}(s)s=\rho^{\prime\prime}(s)-u(s)
are bounded,
due to (R2), it follows that there exists a constant
0<C_{1}<\infty
,
only depending on
P
, such that

\left\|\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}^{T}}\right\|\leq C_{1}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}.

Since
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}<\infty
, it follows by dominated convergence that for
\bm{\xi}
in the neighborhood
N

of
\bm{\xi}(P)
, it holds that

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),

(B.21)

and that
\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}/\partial\bm{\beta}
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
.

Next consider
\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}/\partial\bm{\theta}
.
For each
j=1,\ldots,l
fixed, we have

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\theta_{j}}&=\frac{u^{\prime}(d)}{2d}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\cdot\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\quad+u(d)\cdot\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}).\end{split}

(B.22)

First note that, similar to (
B.20
),

\bm{\xi}\mapsto\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})/\partial\theta_{j}
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
,
for each
\mathbf{s}
fixed such that
d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\neq 0
.
Consider the first term on the right hand side of (
B.22
).
From (
B.13
), we have

\|\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\|\leq d\|\mathbf{X}\|\sqrt{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})}.

Moreover, similar to the reasoning in (
B.15
), we find

\left|(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\right|\leq d^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1}).

(B.23)

For the second term on the right hand side of (
B.22
),
similar to the reasoning in (
B.13
), we have

\begin{split}\left\|\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\right\|^{2}&=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\quad\leq d^{2}\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right)\\
&\quad\leq d^{2}\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}\\
&\quad\leq d^{2}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|^{2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})^{3}.\end{split}

According to (V4), the mapping
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is continuously differentiable.
This means that
\left\|\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\theta_{j}\right\|
is bounded on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Since
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})
is bounded uniformly on
N
and
because
u(s)s=\rho^{\prime}(s)
and
u^{\prime}(s)s^{2}=\rho^{\prime\prime}(s)s-\rho^{\prime}(s)
are bounded,
it follows that there exists a
constant
0<C_{2}<\infty
, only depending on
P
, such that
for
j=1,\ldots,l
,

\left\|\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right\|\leq C_{2}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}.

As before, it follows by dominated convergence that for
\bm{\xi}
in the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
, it holds that

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),

(B.24)

and that
\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}/\partial\bm{\theta}
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
.

Next consider
\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}/\partial\bm{\beta}
, for
j=1,\ldots,l
.
We have

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}&=\frac{u^{\prime}(d)}{d}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\cdot(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\quad-u(d)\cdot 2\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\quad+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)u(d)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}),\end{split}

(B.25)

where
\mathbf{H}_{j}
is defined in (
7.3
).
As before,

\bm{\xi}\mapsto\partial\Psi_{\theta_{j}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})/\partial\bm{\beta}
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
,
for each
\mathbf{s}
fixed such that
d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\neq 0
.
Consider the first term on the right hand side of (
B.25
).
From (
B.18
),

\left|(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\right|\leq d^{2}M_{2}.

(B.26)

Because
u^{\prime}(s)s^{2}
is bounded,
together with (
B.13
),
the norm of the first term on the right hand side of (
B.25
) is bounded
by a constant times
\|\mathbf{X}\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})^{1/2}
.
Similar to (
B.13
), for the second term on the right hand side of (
B.25
),

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\|^{2}&\leq d^{2}\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{T}\right\|_{2}^{2}\\
&\leq kd^{2}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}\|\mathbf{H}_{j}\|^{2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})^{3},\end{split}

and for the third term on the right hand side of (
B.25
),
we can use (
B.13
) and (
B.17
).
As before, since
u^{\prime}(s)s=\rho^{\prime\prime}(s)-u(s)
and
u(s)s^{2}=\rho^{\prime}(s)s
are bounded, it follows that there exists a
constant
0<C_{3}<\infty
, only depending on
P
,
such that for
j=1,\ldots,l
,

\left\|\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}\right\|\leq C_{3}\|\mathbf{X}\|.

Since
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|<\infty
, if follows by dominated convergence that for
\bm{\xi}
in the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
, it holds that

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),

(B.27)

and that
\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}/\partial\bm{\beta}
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
.

Finally, consider
\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}/\partial\theta_{t}
, for
j,t=1,\ldots,l
.
We find

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}}{\partial\theta_{t}}=&-\frac{u^{\prime}(d)}{2d}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\quad-u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\quad+u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{H}_{j}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\cdot\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\quad-u(d)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\qquad+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\left(\rho(d)-b_{0}\right)\\
&\qquad-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\cdot\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}\theta_{t}}\right)\right)\left(\rho(d)-b_{0}\right)\\
&\qquad+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\cdot\frac{u(d)}{2}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}).\end{split}

(B.28)

As before, together with (V5),

\bm{\xi}\mapsto\partial\Psi_{\theta_{j}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})/\partial\theta_{t}
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
,
for each
\mathbf{s}
fixed such that
d(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P))\neq 0
.
From (
B.15
) and (
B.18
), it follows that the first term
on the right hand side
of (
B.28
) is bounded by

\frac{|u^{\prime}(d)d^{3}|}{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})M_{2}.

Because
u^{\prime}(s)s^{3}=\rho^{\prime\prime}(s)s^{2}-\rho^{\prime}(s)s
is bounded,
together with (
B.16
), we conclude this is
bounded on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Similar to (
B.13
),
the second term on the right hand side
of (
B.28
) is bounded by

|u(d)|d^{2}\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq|u(d)|d^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right\|^{2}\left\|\mathbf{H}_{j}\right\|^{2}\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)^{4}.

Since
u(s)s^{2}=\rho^{\prime}(s)s
is bounded,
together with (
B.17
),
we again find that this is bounded on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
The same holds for the fourth term on the right hand side
of (
B.28
).

We continue with the third term on the right hand side
of (
B.28
).
Similar to (
B.13
),
this is bounded by

|u(d)|d^{2}\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\frac{\partial\mathbf{H}_{j}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1/2}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq|u(d)|d^{2}\lambda_{1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{H}_{j}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right\|.

We have that

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\mathbf{H}_{j}}{\partial\theta_{t}}&=\left\{\mathrm{tr}\left(-\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}\theta_{t}}\right)\right\}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{l}\theta_{s}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right)\\
&\quad+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\left\{\left(\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right)+\left(\sum_{s=1}^{l}\theta_{s}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}\theta_{t}}\right)\right\}\\
&\quad-\left\{\mathrm{tr}\left(-\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{l}\theta_{s}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right)+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{l}\theta_{s}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}\theta_{t}}\right)\right\}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\\
&\quad-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{l}\theta_{s}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right)\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}\theta_{t}}\right).\end{split}

With (
B.5
), (
B.6
), and (
B.16
), we find

\begin{split}\left|\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right)\right|&\leq k\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right\|_{2}\\
&\leq k\left\|\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right\|\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right\|\\
&\leq k\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})M_{1}^{2},\end{split}

(B.29)

which is uniformly bounded on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
,
and similar to (
B.17
) we find

\left|\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}\theta_{t}}\right)\right|\leq k^{3/2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})\left\|\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}\theta_{t}}\right\|.

(B.30)

Because, according to (V5), the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is twice continuously differentiable,
it follows that

\left\|\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}/\partial\theta_{j}\theta_{t}\right\|

is uniformly bounded on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Together with the fact that with (
B.16
),

\left\|\sum_{s=1}^{l}\theta_{s}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right\|\leq\sum_{s=1}^{l}\|\theta_{s}\|\left\|\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{s}}\right\|\leq M_{1}\sum_{s=1}^{l}\|\theta_{s}\|,

it follows that the first term of
\partial\mathbf{H}_{j}/\partial\theta_{t}

is bounded on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
The traces in the other terms can be handled in the same way, which yields that

\|\partial\mathbf{H}_{j}/\partial\theta_{t}\|
is bounded on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Because
u(s)s^{2}=\rho^{\prime}(s)s
is bounded, it follows that the third term
on the right hand side of (
B.28
) is bounded.

Next, consider the fifth term on the right hand side of (
B.28
).
From (
B.29
),

\left|\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{t}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\right|\leq k\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})M_{1}^{2},

which is uniformly bounded on the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Because
\rho(s)
is bounded, it follows that
the fifth term on the right hand side of (
B.28
) is bounded.
For the sixth term on the right hand side of (
B.28
),
from (
B.30
) we have

\left|\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}\theta_{t}}\right)\right|\leq k^{3/2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{-1})\left\|\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}\theta_{t}}\right\|.

Because
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is twice continuously differentiable and
\rho(s)
is bounded,
we conclude that
the sixth term on the right hand side of (
B.28
) is bounded.
Finally, from (
B.17
) and (
B.23
) together with the fact that

u(s)s^{2}=\rho^{\prime}(s)s
is bounded,
it also follows that
the last term on the right hand side of (
B.28
) is bounded.
By putting everything together, it follows that there exists a
constant
0<C_{4}<\infty
, only depending on
P
,
such that
for
j,t=1,\ldots,l
,

\left\|\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\theta_{t}}\right\|\leq C_{4}.

It follows by dominated convergence that for
\bm{\xi}
in the neighborhood
N
of
\bm{\xi}(P)
, it holds

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),

(B.31)

and that
\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}/\partial\bm{\theta}
is continuous at
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

For convenience we state the following result from
[
17
]

about spherically contoured densities, see Lemma 5.1 in
[
17
]
.
This lemma uses the commutation matrix
\mathbf{K}_{k,k}
, which
is the
k^{2}\times k^{2}
block matrix with the
(i,j)
-block being equal to the
k\times k
matrix
\mathbf{\Delta}_{ji}

consisting of zero’s except a 1 at entry
(j,i)
.
A useful property (e.g., see
[
20
, Section 3.7]
) is that for any
k\times k
matrix
\mathbf{A}
,
it holds that

\mathbf{K}_{k,k}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{A})=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{A}^{T}).

(B.32)

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{z}
has a
k
-variate elliptical contoured density
defined in (
3.2
), with parameters
\bm{\mu}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{I}_{k}
.
Then
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{z}/\|\mathbf{z}\|
is independent of
\|\mathbf{z}\|
,
has mean zero and covariance matrix
(1/k)\mathbf{I}_{k}
.
Furthermore,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{T}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{T})\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{T})^{T}=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{I}_{k^{2}}+\mathbf{K}_{k,k})+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{I}_{k})\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{I}_{k})^{T},

where
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=(k(k+2))^{-1}
.

Proof of Lemma
2

Proof.

Write
\partial\Lambda/\partial\bm{\xi}
as in (
B.19
).
We determine each block separately and apply Lemma
B.3
.
Let
\bm{\xi}_{P}=(\bm{\beta}_{P},\bm{\theta}_{P})=(\bm{\beta}(P),\bm{\theta}(P))
.
When we also write
\mathbf{V}_{P}
instead of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(P))
, then according to Lemma
B.3

we have

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}_{P})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}&=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&=-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(d)}{d}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}\mathbf{X}+u(d)\cdot\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right]\\
&=-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(d)}{d}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\mu})(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\mu})^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X}+u(d)\cdot\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\,\Bigg|\,\mathbf{X}\right]\right],\\
\end{split}

where
d^{2}=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})
.
The inner expectation on the right hand side is the conditional expectation of
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
,
which has the same distribution as
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbf{z}+\bm{\mu}
,
where
\mathbf{z}
has a spherical density
f_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}
.
This implies that the inner expectation on the right hand side is equal to

\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)}{\|\mathbf{z}\|}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{T}+u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\mathbf{I}_{k}\right]\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}.

From Lemma
B.4
, we find

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)}{\|\mathbf{z}\|}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{T}+u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\mathbf{I}_{k}\right]&=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|\frac{\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{T}}{\|\mathbf{z}\|^{2}}+u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\mathbf{I}_{k}\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\Big[u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|\Big]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{T}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\right]\mathbf{I}_{k}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{I}_{k}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\right]\mathbf{I}_{k}\\
&=\alpha\mathbf{I}_{k},\end{split}

where
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{z}/\|\mathbf{z}\|
and

\alpha=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\frac{1}{k}u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|+u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)\frac{\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)}{\|\mathbf{z}\|}+\frac{1}{k}\rho^{\prime\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\right].

It follows that

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}_{P})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}=-\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right].

Next, for
j=1,\ldots,l
, consider

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}_{P})}{\partial\theta_{j}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})}{\partial\theta_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(d)}{2d}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}_{P}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})\cdot\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})\,\Bigg|\,\mathbf{X}\right]\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[u(d)\cdot\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}_{P}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})\,\Bigg|\,\mathbf{X}\right]\right].\end{split}

(B.33)

According to Lemma
B.4
, the inner conditional expectation of the first term
on the right hand side of (
B.33
) can be written as

\begin{split}&\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)}{2\|\mathbf{z}\|}\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}_{P}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{z}\right]\\
&=\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|^{2}}{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\mathbf{u}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}_{P}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{u}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{u}\right],\end{split}

Since the second term on the right hand side is the expectation with respect to a spherical density of an odd function of
\mathbf{u}
,
this expectation is equal
to zero due to Lemma
B.4
.
Similarly, the second term on the right hand side of (
B.33
)
has inner conditional expectation

\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}_{P}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{z}\right]=\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}_{P}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\mathbf{u}\right]=\mathbf{0},

due to Lemma
B.4
.
It follows that

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}_{P})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}=\mathbf{0}.

Next, using Lemma
B.3
and (
B.25
),
for all
j=1,\ldots,l
, consider

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta},j}}{\partial\bm{\beta}^{T}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P})}{\partial\bm{\beta}^{T}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&=-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(d)}{d}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})\cdot\,(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})\,\Bigg|\,\mathbf{X}\right]\right]\\
&\quad-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[u(d)\cdot 2\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})\,\Bigg|\,\mathbf{X}\right]\right]\\
&\quad-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\theta_{j}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\rho^{\prime}(d)}{d}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}_{P}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{P})\,\Bigg|\,\mathbf{X}\right]\right].\end{split}

(B.34)

According to Lemma
B.4
,
the first term on the right hand side of (
B.34
)
has inner conditional expectation

\begin{split}&\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\frac{u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)}{\|\mathbf{z}\|}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{z}\right]\\
&\qquad=\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|^{2}\right]\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{u}\right].\end{split}

Again, the second term on the right hand side is the expectation with respect to a spherical density
of an odd function of
\mathbf{u}
, and is therefore equal to zero.
Similarly, the inner expectation of the second
term on the right hand side of (
B.34
) is equal to

2\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\mathbf{z}\right]=2\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{j}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[u(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\mathbf{u}\right]=\mathbf{0},

and the inner expectation of the third
term on the right hand side of (
B.34
) is equal to

\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\frac{\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)}{\|\mathbf{z}\|}\mathbf{z}\right]=\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\right]\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\mathbf{u}\right]=\mathbf{0}.

It follows that

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{\xi}_{P})}{\partial\bm{\beta}}=\mathbf{0}.

Finally,
to determine
\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\theta},j}(\bm{\xi}_{P})/\partial\theta_{s}
,
note that when
\mathbf{V}
is linear, we can write

\Psi_{\bm{\theta},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})=-\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)^{T}\mathrm{vec}\left(\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi})\right)

where
\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}
is defined in (
7.8
) and has the property that
